# *Introduction*

For many years I considered writing a book to help people understand the path of Tantra (which, in a phrase, may be defined as “the all-round struggle for self-realization”).[^1] But I shied away from the task, sure that a suitable book must already exist. However, though there are libraries of books about meditation or occult power or yoga, I was never able to find a book that explains the modern Tantric experience.

Today’s Tantrics can no longer enjoy solitary life in the jungles or caves. In the past, yogis were free to work solely on the psychic level but today social conditions are so grave that they have to assist on every level, physical as well as psychic. Confronted by the global ecological crisis, they understand its cause: a technological society which is destroying itself by its greed for money and power. They also recognize that an age of spiritual awareness is coming, and that this crisis is like the darkness that comes before the dawn, a darkness which is increasingly compelling humankind to wake up from its long sleep.

Most people believe that yogic meditation and Tantra are, at best, self-centered practices for procuring one’s own personal peace and, at worst, sects concerned with achieving occult powers or indulging in animalistic orgies. Nothing could be further from the truth, but no one can blame the public for having these misconceptions because these are the most publicized images of meditation and Tantra.

When I started writing this book, my aim was to reveal the lesser known but truer Tantra, that Tantra whose real concern is the welfare of the entire creation. It began as a work of fiction based on my personal experiences. I thought that this would protect the book from criticism by those who might think I was

***

[^1]: For a more detailed definition of the words *Tantra* and *yoga*, please see both the glossary and the appendix entitled *Tantra, Veda and Yoga*.

trying to publicize the organization to which I belong. But when I began getting feedback on my rough drafts, nearly everyone encouraged me to simply tell the facts as they actually took place rather than cloak them in a fictional guise.

I therefore turned to straight autobiography, basing it on many years of personal diaries. And I did not avoid mention of my organization which is called *Ananda Marga*, or its founder and master, Shrii Shrii Anandamurti, who is informally called “Baba”. I must admit to a hidden motivation: I wanted as many people as possible to know about Baba, and the vast work for which He sacrificed every drop of His energy. I have staked my own life on His teachings because I am convinced that they are vital for achieving global cooperation between good people, good ideas and good organizations so that we may overcome the present crisis and usher in a new socio-spiritual era.[^2]

Throughout Baba’s life (He physically died in 1990), only those who were practicing His meditation were able to get some sort of picture of Him. Though His life was dedicated to serving the world, He kept himself completely away from the public. He never appeared on television nor did He allow any reporter to talk to Him. During the last twenty years of His life, He preferred not even to meet anyone who was not practicing meditation. Once, after repeated requests for Him to write His autobiography, He handed a piece of paper to His disciples and said, “Here is my autobiography.” The paper read: “I was a mystery. I am a mystery. I will always remain a mystery.” Though I cannot hope to solve that mystery, I feel impelled to do my best to expose to you, the reader, something of the Baba that I knew and know. (Along with my personal perspective of Baba, I have included a short chronological explanation of His life. It immediately follows this *Introduction*.)

Here, then, are my own experiences with Baba, with His

[^2]: Toward the end of this Introduction, an explanation is given why pronouns referring to Baba are capitalized.

work and with His disciples. Much of it I have never mentioned to anyone. Though my life has been extraordinary, it is not unusual. Rather, the extraordinary is the norm among dedicated spiritualists. I know hundreds of Tantrics who could write a book like this and, in fact, one of the motivations behind this book is to inspire those Tantrics to write their own stories.

As you read, you will see that I do not restrain myself from including the harsher or more confusing sides of the path. I think the reader deserves a complete picture rather than the popular misconceptions that the path is only “beautiful”, or that the spiritual master is only “sweet”. I have gone so far as to describe major controversies about the Ananda Marga organization. I also portray the extremes of Baba’s ‘anger’, the occasional severe stinging of his speech, even His use of a stick to beat His disciples.

The authoritative Tantra scripture, *Tantrasara*, describes the qualities of the best Gurus. It includes this statement:

*Nigrahánugrahe shakto gururityabhidhiiyate*

This means, “The guru must possess the capacity both to punish, and to love, or bless, his disciples.”

Moreover, I have not hidden my own apparent idolization of the Guru—which I prefer to see as a desire to dive as deeply as possible into the experience of His vast personality.

Many of the stories about Baba are not exceptional—rather they are typical. It is for this very reason that they are included—to give you a general idea of Baba’s lifestyle and way of speaking, and to suggest, at least in some way, the immeasurable extent of His knowledge. For example, some stories allude to the fact that He understood and spoke every language and every dialect of the world. Others include examples of the occult demonstrations and cures He sometimes performed for His disciples. All in all, I selected only a fraction

of the many incidents I witnessed.

This account, which starts at the age of eighteen, contains no reference to my origins or earlier life as a youth. A few lines should suffice. I was brought up in a middle-cum-upper-class suburb north of Chicago. My father was a trial lawyer, and my mother a housewife and an oil-painter. Both were active in the civil rights and anti-war movements; in the latter years of their lives, they invested much of themselves in the fight against capital punishment. My only sibling, a sister, achieved a fair degree of international fame as a cellist in a top-notch classical trio. The most extraordinary member of our family was our dog, Judy, whom I once calculated knew one hundred and fifteen words, responding appropriately to each of them even when they were spoken in a monotone. For example, at the faintest mention of the word *bath*, she would raise her ears and then dive to safety behind the sofa. Judy once indicated to me that the best place for meditation was actually right there, behind the sofa, where no one could disturb her, though I believe she was joking.

I was an above-average student, leading a normal extra-curricular and social life. This included experimenting with most of the usual vices and indulging in mild forms of revolutionary expression. While studying, I also tried many jobs, each for a few months, including working as a lawyer’s investigator, an accountant, a government census-taker, a postman, a construction worker, a supermarket clerk, a newspaper delivery boy, and many varied jobs on a golf course. Throughout all my early experiences, I was keenly aware that none of them were fully satisfying. An insatiable appetite for new tastes and new knowledge continually drove me from one thing to another. While attending high school, I once wrote in an essay: “If I had to summarize my personal philosophy in two sentences I would say: 1) Maximally utilize every moment, and then pass on to the next. 2) Savor every last baked bean.”

The book also makes short work of explaining the socio-spiritual disciplines and groups that I joined before finally entering Ananda Marga. Most of the people who read my drafts expressed interest in reading about those experiences but the fact is that they were mostly not so important, and so I abbreviated that section.

I also do not include many incidents concerned with my social work, though that work takes up about half of my time. I prefer to relate only those experiences which are colorful, profound, exciting, or give some insight into Baba’s personality and my relationship to him. To fill this gap, I occasionally mention the beginnings of certain new projects, or describe experiences that took place during this work.

Another missing element is the absence of any explanation about our socio-economic concept, *Prout*—which is an acronym for the *Progressive Utilization Theory*. To get a minimal understanding of Ananda Marga, some exposure to the meaning of Prout is needed. For this purpose, a brief introduction to Prout is contained in the appendices.

A few entries have somewhat technical content, for example, regarding agriculture, economy and languages. I have also included something on the history of Ananda Marga. The reader may or may not be interested in these topics, so they have been placed in the appendices.

Some sections of the book are detailed, while others sketchy. In some places, I offer philosophical explanations, and elsewhere I intentionally leave you in the dark.

All incidents were personally witnessed by me, unless I mention otherwise. As to Baba’s quoted speech, in many instances I was quick enough to exactly record His words. At other times, I was forced to write them down afterward according to memory.

Regarding *Sanskrit* terms, I use only those few which are most common and thus valuable to know. Throughout the text these words are italicized, except when they appear with great

frequency. The first time any such term appears it is usually defined in parentheses or in a footnote. At the end of the text is a glossary of the Sanskrit words which appear in more than one diary entry. This glossary also includes other Special English terminology and abbreviations.

There is one additional point that I feel is important. In my original diaries I capitalized the pronoun, “He”, wherever it referred to Baba. I have maintained this practice throughout the book. This may appear to be either a blasphemy or the expression of a blind belief. But this is a principle and even a practice of Tantra, to give the highest regard to the spiritual master or *guru*. Without the readiness to do anything and everything He tells us to do, one cannot progress rapidly on the path. If a small child doubts everything his teacher tells him, it will be not only absurd but highly unproductive.

Imagine you are a passenger, and the guru is driver of the car. As long as you hesitate to get in the car, the driver is handicapped, even if you momentarily sit down. If you stay in the car, but keep the door open, ready to leap out if the going gets dangerous, the driver also cannot go ahead except at a very slow pace. Only if you close the door and lock it firmly, will the driver be ready to take you forward at the proper speed. You may be frightened by the speed but the driver knows what he is doing.

By capitalizing the pronoun, the guru is equated to God. The ancient Vedas (which, though not infallible, do contain a wealth of knowledge) ask a question: If God and guru are standing before the disciple, whose feet should the disciple touch first? The answer is that the disciple should touch the guru’s feet first. The explanation is that the guru is the way to God, without whom it would be impossible to ever meet God. The disciple treats the guru not as an ordinary teacher, but as the spiritual path itself. Why? If we learn a *mantra* or other meditation technique from the guru, and practice it with full concentration, are we not, in effect, surrendering to the guru?

The good or bad value of the mantra depends on the guru. The practice is subtle and powerful, and is designed, after all, to alter the mind itself. If, then, we do the practice but believe its source to be faulty, it is both hypocritical and useless.

The best attitude for the spiritual aspirant is the one hundred percent self-honesty which admits, “I know nothing.” I cannot know for certain whether anything is right or wrong, what to speak of judging my spiritual teacher. If there is a God, He knows this incompleteness in me even better than I know it. Yet, He created in my life conditions which demand judgment and decisive determination, without which I am not even able to rise from my bed in the morning.

We fool ourselves if we think that we do not judge. In many ways, every day, we judge. And yet we do not know for sure if our judgments are correct. Those who say, “I keep my mind completely open; I neither judge anyone nor believe in anything”—say one thing and do another.

Tantra understands that we may doubt our judgments, but, nevertheless, we should proceed ahead as if there is no doubt. We should implement our decisions with full commitment. To reap the real benefit from the guru, the disciple must treat Him as the Way to God—the Word of God. If you say, “But that’s very risky!” the fact is not changed that we are compelled to judge every moment without understanding in the deepest sense that which we judge. The fact also remains that, to achieve anything great, a great risk is required. There is no greater risk than that of the spiritual path. Yet the risk is only an apparent one, because God cannot expect more from us than our best. As far as He is concerned, our best is perfect.

I speak of my guru as “He”, confident that God appreciates the thought behind it. Because God *is* the thought behind it.

# *A Brief Biography of Shrii Shrii Anandamurtiji*

Shrii Shrii Anandamurtiji was born in Jamalpur, Bihar, India, at dawn on the same day that the Buddha was born about 2500 years earlier, that is, the full-moon day in the Bengali month of Vaishak (April-May) in 1922.[^1]

Because the sun was rising at the moment of His birth, the baby was named Arun, which means "crimson dawn". Later, His name was changed to Prabhat Ranjan, meaning "that which colors the dawn". His full legal name was Prabhat Ranjan Sarkar.

Some days after the baby's birth, a ceremony was performed at which many of the family members were present. A cotton wick was dipped into a silver pot of milk and then held over the baby's mouth so that the milk could drip in. At that moment, however, Arun lurched forward, grabbed the

[^1]: In 1922, the full moon fell on May 11.

wick and started to drink from it directly. Everyone was shocked, especially the grandmother who exclaimed, “He is not a baby, rather he is a grown-up boy! He is Burho!” From that moment, *Burho*, which means “the ancient one”, became His nickname. Later on it was shortened to *Bubu*. Many years later, when Baba was asked about this incident, He said it was at that time He realized that it would be better for Him to act like a normal child.

When He was only an infant, He narrated strange night-time experiences to His mother, telling her how all the animals of the universe would enter in one of His ears, and go out the other. The descriptions fit real animals, most of which He had never seen. He also narrated how all the planets and galaxies were floating through Him. His mother became so concerned that she brought Him to several mystics, and asked them to use their powers to understand what was happening with her son. But no one could give her a satisfactory answer.

His family members all remember seeing Prabhat Ranjan, even from the time He was a very young child, frequently sitting on His bed in the middle of the night performing meditation.

Because He spent much of His time lying in bed, His sister one day complained that He was a lazy child. She said she believed that He did not even know how to write His name. When she produced a pen and paper to test Him, He wrote His name in five scripts: Bengali, Arabic, Roman, Devanagrii and Tamil. From that moment she stopped pestering Him.

Many years later, Baba mentioned that at that young age, while spending long hours lying in His bed, He chalked out His blueprint for the future Ananda Marga.

When He was five years old, Prabhat Ranjan accompanied His parents to a Shiva temple. In the presence of the temple priest, the child gracefully recited a lengthy Sanskrit hymn to Shiva with perfect accent and intonation. The priest was shocked. How could a small, uneducated boy, without any

prior exposure to Sanskrit, perform such a feat? His parents were also awe-struck by their own child.

When admitted to the Jamalpur primary school, Prabhat Ranjan caught everyone’s interest by His astonishing memory and grasp of countless scientific phenomena and geographic facts that were obviously beyond the capacity of a human mind, what to speak of a child’s mind.

He also surprised many people by His daily habit of visiting the old dilapidated Kali Hill Temple, a thoroughly frightening place which everyone else avoided. When asked why He went there, the child replied, “I go there to think.”

One day, while walking home from school, He came upon a group of other students standing on the road. A large bull blocked the path of the children, and they were afraid to push it aside. Prabhat Ranjan stepped forward, held His palm in front of the bull’s forehead, and immediately the bull sat down.

As a student, Prabhat Ranjan led the others in every aspect. During classes, He sat very still, listening carefully, and absorbing every detail. But once out of class he would change completely. He loved gymnastics, swimming, wrestling, football, track and field events and other sports. He also enjoyed playing the flute, and writing poetry and short stories. After a few years, He composed articles concerned with public welfare, which were often published in popular magazines.

In 1939, He entered a Calcutta college, where He developed a reputation for assisting students troubled by poverty. He took private tutoring jobs just to earn money to help others. Students also flocked to Him for help in their studies—even senior students. He also began attracting many people with His unusual talents—palm-reading, fortune-telling, and manifesting various supra-psychic phenomena.

Throughout His life, Baba never had any spiritual teacher or guru. One night, however, at the age of sixteen, He began His own work as a guru. It was a full-moon night, and He was meditating as usual in a cremation ground on the bank of the

Ganges. Suddenly, He heard a rough voice demanding, “Give me your money, or I’ll kill you just now!” Slowly turning around, He found Himself facing a tall, robust criminal. He calmly said, “Kalicharan, I shall certainly give you all the money I have. But first tell me whether you rob people out of necessity or out of habit.”

The dreaded thief was electrified by the composure of the slight lad, and amazed that he had been addressed by name. In a flash he understood that the youth was a saint. Kalicharan said, “All my life I wanted to be a good person, but was never given the chance.”

Prabhat Ranjan replied, “I will give you that chance right now. Make yourself ready, and I will teach you meditation.” Without another word, Kalicharan bathed in the river, then sat down and said, “*Khoka*, teach me as you want.” *Khoka* means little boy. Prabhat Ranjan corrected him, “Call me Baba.” Then He initiated Kalicharan who, while doing meditation, entered into trance. Afterwards, Baba compelled a weeping Kalicharan to accept the few coins He had in His pocket. From that day, the rectified thief became a great spiritualist and his name was changed to Kalikananda.

During His time in Calcutta, Baba stayed at the house of His maternal uncle, Sarat Chandra Basu. Sarat Chandra’s cousin was the famous social activist, Subhash Chandra Bose. Though the name of Subhash Chandra Bose is not so familiar throughout the non-Indian world, in India his memory is commonly given equal or greater respect than that of Mahatma Gandhi. These two figures were the greatest leaders of the movement to gain independence from England. Another renowned personality with whom Baba had a close relationship was the revolutionary sociologist, M.N. Roy. Both Subhash Chandra Bose and M.N. Roy frequently visited Baba to discuss sociological issues with Him. Subhash Chandra also benefited from Baba’s knowledge of Tantra.

In 1941, the dire financial condition of His family forced

Baba to give up His further studies. He returned home and joined the accounts department of the railway workshop in Jamalpur—the biggest such workshop in all of Asia, with thousands of employees. Two years later, during the Second World War, He entered the Territorial Army. After completing His military service, Baba returned to the railway workshop, where he continued working for more than twenty years. He was esteemed by the staff for His perfect efficiency and loving nature. Moreover, He was known as a palmist and fortuneteller. Many people came to Him to find out the whereabouts of their lost children and articles, and also to be healed from chronic or incurable ailments.

One day, Prabhat Ranjan was approached by an Englishman who told Him that his wife was suffering in a London hospital. She had sent a telegram saying that the doctors found it difficult to diagnose her disease, but had decided to remove one of her kidneys. The man was depressed because he could not go to England to comfort his wife due to the war. Baba closed His eyes, and then told him to send a cable requesting the doctors to make another medical check-up. Baba said, “Do not worry. A simple operation will suffice. Your wife will be cured and will soon return to you.” After a few days, news came that his wife was healed and on her way to India. When she arrived, Baba was invited to their house. When He entered, the wife was so astonished she dropped the tray of teacups. She took her husband into the side room and asked, “Who is this gentleman?” The husband said, “It is Shrii Prabhat Ranjan Sarkar, my sole friend when I was in distress about your illness.” She became dumbfounded and then replied, “It is impossible, because he is the same Indian doctor who prevailed on the other doctors not to remove my kidney but to opt for a minor operation! When the operation was performed, he remained all the time next to me, keeping his hand on my head. It made me feel completely calm.” The husband was stunned. When he asked Baba about it, Baba

simply replied, “How could it be possible?”

In those days, Baba kept a special mirror in His bedroom. Occasionally, when people expressed anxiety to Him about their faraway relatives, He would allow them to look into the mirror and see their relatives. From this experience they felt great relief. Each time that He did this, however, He became sick. A woman was once desperately weeping because she was unable to meet her mother just before the old lady’s death. Baba showed her the mirror in which she saw her mother calmly sitting in a rowboat on a lake. After this, He became sick for an entire month. Baba’s mother then said, “No more!” and destroyed the mirror.

Until 1954, Baba led a life of spiritual camouflage. He initiated a large number of people without letting one another know that they were the disciples of one and the same guru. Most of His co-workers and even His family did not know about His spiritual work. On November 7, 1954, He called many of His disciples together for the first time and delivered His first spiritual address. On January 9, 1955, Ananda Marga Pracaraka Samgha was formally founded. He explained that the organization aimed at a two-fold ideal: liberation of self, and service to the world. Then, for the first time at a collective meeting, Baba gave His now-famous *Barabhaya mudra*—a powerful hand-gesture, which caused everyone to enter into spiritual trance.

In the late 1950’s Baba married, and later a baby boy was born. Thus Baba demonstrated that a family was no impediment to a life of supreme dedication.

From 1955, Baba began training spiritual teachers or *acharyas* and empowering them to teach the meditation lessons. In the initial years, all of these men and women were respected and well-educated family people. They eventually numbered several hundred. In these first years of Ananda Marga, Baba also wrote much of the basic spiritual and social philosophy. He saturated His disciples in blissful experiences,

and gave almost no guidance regarding any social work, except for the propagation of spiritual and yoga practices. Baba frequently demonstrated truly extraordinary psycho-spiritual phenomena. He induced different states of superconsciousness in His disciples, made them hear the divine sounds, and even caused individuals to die and then brought them back to life.

This purely spiritual phase ended in 1962, when Baba began the order of monks and nuns, or *Dadas* and *Didis*. From then on, Ananda Marga’s growth accelerated greatly, and it began to spread throughout India. Though His demonstrations continued, Baba now began organizing massive social service programs. In 1963, the Education, Relief and Welfare Section began. Workers and *Margiis* (members of Ananda Marga) threw themselves into catastrophe relief work, and opened schools and welfare homes.

Yet it was only at the end of 1966, when the organization had grown to immense proportions, that Baba agreed to give up His job at the railway office. By maintaining His employment throughout the foundation years of the organization, He demonstrated that busy family people are capable of both spiritual achievement and service to society. He accepted His workers’ request to give up His job only when they promised to keep up with His speed, which He said would increase ten times. Baba moved to Ananda Nagar, our global model master unit, located in an impoverished tribal area. The organization’s speed then became something unimaginable.

Throughout the same period, the public was exposed to Baba’s socio-economic concept called Progressive Utilization Theory (or Prout), which He had first given in 1959. Proutist ideas began to spread rapidly, as its members worked against various pockets of corruption. Opposition to Ananda Marga developed among corrupt politicians and other shady public figures. Eventually in 1967, an attack was organized against Ananda Nagar by members of the local Communist Party. Five monks were murdered. Several politicians and hoodlums were

eventually arrested and convicted for the murders.

Other serious incidents occurred over the following years, including one more murder by the Communists in 1969.[^2]

By that time, Ananda Marga’s influence had grown enormously. Half the police commissioners of Bihar state, as well as many other public officials, were Margiis. The Margiis gained a reputation as scrupulously honest people who refused to accept bribes or in any way compromise their morality. Baba had always spoken out against all forms of corruption, and Margiis in public positions began exposing the corruption rampant in the administration.

Because of these developments, in 1969, the federal government passed a ban order, forbidding civil servants and other government employees from joining Ananda Marga. The order claimed that Ananda Marga was a political organization. Ananda Marga then filed a challenge in the Supreme Court and won the case, causing the ban to be withdrawn.

Desperate to stop Ananda Marga, the government, in complicity with the Central Bureau of Investigation (CBI), resorted to drastic measures. They concocted murder conspiracy charges against Baba, and He was arrested in December 1971. The so-called victims’ bodies presented to support the charges were mutilated and unidentifiable. Though there was no real evidence, the case dragged on for nearly seven years, while Baba lived in pitiful jail conditions. In 1973, when it appeared that we might soon win the case, an attempt was made to kill Baba by poison. He lost His eyesight and underwent intense pain for many days. When the government refused to investigate the poisoning, Baba began a protest fast, drinking only two cups of liquid daily—which He continued for more than five years. He stopped the fast when He was

[^2]: It should be understood that the Communists were, even then, a major political force in India. And until very recently they continued to hold the power in West Bengal, the state in which Ananda Nagar and Calcutta are both located. Our central office was in Calcutta.

proven innocent, honorably acquitted and released from jail in August 1978.

During the time of His fasting, several Members of Parliament came to visit Baba. This was one of the few known times after 1966 that He permitted non-Margiis to meet and talk to Him—He otherwise remained inaccessible to the general public. The visiting officials begged Baba to break His fast, arguing that His life was vital for the success of His mission. But Baba replied, “My ideas are more precious than my life.”

On the other hand, when He was later asked how it was possible that He sustained His body despite prolonged fasting, He answered, “There is nothing unnatural about it. The only difference is that while other people take energy assimilated in their edibles, I have to derive energy directly from sunlight.”

Before Baba was imprisoned, Ananda Marga was active in only five countries. By the time He came out, it had spread to eighty, and had become the world’s largest traditional yoga movement. In accordance with Tantric principles, the struggles undergone by the workers and Margiis had only helped to strengthen them.

Such difficulties, however, were far from finished. In 1982, seventeen Dadas and Didis were simultaneously killed in a barbarous manner by the Communists in Calcutta. Later, Dada Ajitananda was beaten to death in jail because he refused to support a false case filed by the Communists.

In 1981, Baba conducted an extraordinary three-month program in which He used His subtle perception to analyze the conduct and health of thousands of Margiis, one by one. It was a unique activity never before done by any spiritual master. This was the only time in Baba’s life that He clearly exposed His occult power to such a large number of people over many days continuously.

From 1985, a massive development program of Ananda Nagar was undertaken. More than one hundred small and large

buildings were constructed, farms were started, various livelihood training programs for the neighboring villagers were begun, ecological energy systems were established, women’s welfare activities were undertaken, agricultural research stations were created, and the network of roads and rivers was greatly expanded. A hospital was built, which served hundreds of people every week. The kindergarten, primary school, high school and university had a total enrollment of over one thousand students, many living in hostels and children’s homes. A bakery was started along with several other small industries.

The Communists, intimidated by such progressive activities in the center of a belt of poor, illiterate tribal people, began striking directly at Ananda Nagar. Almost every day, thugs attempted to destroy buildings or crops, or to attack our workers. Ananda Marga’s leading agricultural scientist, Dada Asiimananda, was murdered along with four other workers. Later, one worker was killed and four Margiis were severely injured when they were trying to protest police mistreatment of a number of overseas Margiis. All of these disturbances continued until the day that Baba left His physical body. After that, the attacks greatly diminished; and eventually they stopped.

Baba left behind a vast legacy. He wrote over 200 books on diverse subjects. An incomplete list of those subjects is as follows:

• spiritual philosophy and practice, yoga and Tantra
• psychic development
• the cycle of creation and reincarnation
• social philosophy, norms, ceremonies and systems
• Bengali dictionary of over 6000 difficult words, with derivations
• Bengali encyclopedia of over 6000 pages
• English, Sanskrit and Bengali grammar books
• language, script and philology

• microvita (most minuscule and mysterious life form)
• Neo-humanism (overcoming dogma, creating universalism by devotion)
• agriculture
• health habits and medical treatment
• economics
• education
• justice and women’s rights
• the judicial system and criminality
• culture, literature and fine arts
• industrial policy, cooperatives and commerce
• ecology, population growth and decentralization
• politics, government, democracy, communism and progressive socialism
• history and civilization: socially, economically, culturally and spiritually
• analysis of spiritual scriptures and mythological writings
• analysis of major religious schools and historically-related figures
• morality
• bio-psychology, glands and anatomy
• the social roles of the major professions
• animals
• short stories
• children’s stories
• dramas

In addition to these prolific writings, Baba composed 5018 devotional songs in eight languages, collectively called *Prabhat Sangiit*. The songs were written during the last eight years of His life. These were not little jingles with simple melodies, but rather of such a high quality that they were praised by India’s leading classical singers, some of whom bemoaned the fact that they had never met Baba.

Baba left His physical body on October 21, 1990.

# CHAPTER 1

*Hope*

**From darkness to light**

Chicago, 1969. Perfect weather this morning. A vibrant green park complete with swings, merry-go-round, beach balls, a picnic-lunch, and Mark, Richard, Peggy, Laurie and Lynn for company. Ditching school made it so much more spicy. No one here but us. Everybody else stuck at their jobs or in school. Too bad.

Laughing and laughing, jumping on and off the swings and the jungle-gym, tossing the balls, rolling on the ground—we were beyond the freedom of children.

Sitting opposite Peggy on the teeter-totter, Richard was singing:

> *Don’t need no dirty wine.*
> *We’ve got pure sunshine. Do what you like!*
> *Do, do*
> *Do what you like!*
> *Do, do*
> *Do what you like!*
> *Do, do....*

He grabbed Peggy, gave her a quick kiss, then darted a few steps away.

For some reason, I asked Mark for the time. He turned a big grin toward me, “Time? You’re nuts. There’s no time here, brother. We’re in heaven, didn’t you notice?” Then he took off his watch and threw it nonchalantly over his shoulder into the deep grass.

A hot blast of confusion suddenly hit me. What was going on? What were we doing? What was the purpose of it all? Everybody I knew, absolutely everybody, was struggling to get the so-called freedom we had that morning. But it was meaningless as far as I could see. Nowhere. If this was heaven, then something was seriously wrong.

Mark looked at me curiously and started laughing. “Hey, old pal, why so grim? Looks like you caught a bug in your pants. Tell me, what’re you doing—philosophizing or something?”

I had to get out of there. Without thinking, I turned around and started walking toward the car.

“Hey, hey, hey! Stop!” the girls yelled at me, laughing. “You can’t leave our movie!”

“Bummer! He is going to leave,” Mark said. “And it’s *his* car.”

They all ran after me. I opened the driver’s door, got in, and they tumbled in one after another.

Everyone was talking at once as I started the car and drove away.

“What’s this caper?” “You got whammied or what?” “Where’re you going?”

“I’ve got to get out of here.”

“But we want to stay!”

“Suit yourselves.”

“Wow, you’re spoiling everything! What’s going on?”

“Sorry, but this is all going nowhere. None of it makes sense to me any more.... I just don’t understand.”

“Understand what, pal?” asked Mark.

“Anything! What difference does it all make? What are we

all aiming for? Where are we going? The whole world seems to be running toward nothing and we’re no different.”

“Yeah, that’s cool, bro,” Richard answered, “but whatever happened to ‘Kick out the jams’ and ‘Won’t somebody please throw me a cheeseburger’? I mean you’re forgetting some of the superlative elements of unadulterated existentialism.”

“Look,” I said, more to myself than to them. “Here comes a truck. What difference would it make if I just turned the wheel and drove into that truck?” I wasn’t just joking either. I wanted to figure it out while the truck was still in front of us.

“Ah, yeah, yeah,” Mark said quickly. “No difference at all. And it also won’t make any difference if you pull the car over to the side of the road and let me drive, pal.”

Right. It didn’t make any difference, and I didn’t care. But since he seemed to care, I mindlessly slowed the car down and stopped on the roadside. As I got out of the driver’s seat, they all sighed. “Man, that was close!”

By the time I got home, I was even more confused. All I could see was a dark question mark. No purpose in sight. And no one I knew who could offer an answer. The only thing that occurred to me was to drop out of school and go search for someone, anyone, who could help me make a bit of sense out of the world.

I explained to Mom as best I could what I was going through, and informed her that I was going upstairs to pack so I could go find “someone who knows something.”

“What are you talking about, son? Have you gone crazy?”

Maybe I had. But there was no reason to go on hanging around there, caught in meaningless circles, playing useless games twenty-four hours a day.

“Look.... You can’t just do this,” she said. “No.... Your life will fall apart.... And what about school? And....”

“I’m not going to stand here talking about stupid things with you, Mom. What’s the purpose of going to school if I don’t know where I’m going, or for that matter why I should

even continue to exist? No, there’s no reason to delay and please don’t try to stop me.”

“God, this is too much for me!” She was struggling to find the right words. “Okay, I won’t try to stop you. But can’t you at least wait for Dad to get home? It’s not fair just to run away like this without first talking to him.”

She had a point. I agreed to wait until Dad got home, which was still two or three hours away. As I waited, my confusion deepened.

When he arrived, I started to tell him about my condition and my plan. He hardly paid attention. Instead he asked me if I wanted to take a walk.

At the sound of the word “walk”, Judy bounded into the room, her tongue hanging a mile out. She ran to the door and repeatedly jumped on it.

We took a walk in the nearby botanical gardens with Judy running in large circles around us, barking in her short staccato. The gardens were as beautiful as always but I hardly noticed. Again I tried to explain to Dad what I was going through but he could not seem to understand.

We walked to the top of a hill and sat down. Over and over again, Dad threw a stick down the hill for Judy to run after and fetch.

I became absorbed in my own thoughts: Maybe the idea of “meaning” is just a human creation.... Maybe life’s just a dream.... Maybe nothing matters.... What to speak of “God”.... I guess ... I guess there’s no God.... People say so many things, but it proves nothing.... And how can one ever know anyway? We understand *good* according to *bad*, *high* in relation to *low*, *bright* in relation to *dark*, everything in relation to something else.... Nothing has any meaning in itself.... The differences are all just apparent, just relative.... There is no answer, and never will be.... That’s why everybody remains blindly busy doing whatever they feel like.... Of course they’re all afraid of death.... But if I were to die now, what difference would it

make.... No difference to the world.... No difference if I live or die....

The dark thoughts rolled on and on, seemingly endless.

Was it at the nadir of my confusion that I noticed something new creeping into the picture? It’s hard to remember. It was so subtle, almost imperceptible. I couldn’t identify it. As if a cloud covering my thoughts began to thin out and disappear.

I was looking at Judy running down the hill and jumping into the pond to fetch the stick Dad threw. She was laughing, or so it seemed to me. *She’s thoroughly enjoying life*, I thought. I looked around me: the grass, so green, so bright; the leaves of the trees, swaying in the wind; the water rippling. Everything sparkling, everything moving, together—Together!

Without my realizing it, the dark thoughts had vanished. I found myself sliding into a state of mind that I had never experienced before. How can I describe it?

The independent existence of each plant, the dirt, the air, the water, their separateness, disappeared as everything seemed to fuse together, the color of each object merging into that of the one next to it. All colors and forms within one single picture, one single Entity. Even my own body became simply another element within the Whole. I could see my thoughts passing like birds or the shining ripples of water. Each object was as valid, as vitally important to the Whole as any other. Which was “alive”? Which “inanimate”? These distinctions no longer made sense. Instead, everything appeared alive. Everything was joyous, conscious. Like the colors of a dream in which every dream-form was a part of the consciousness of the dreamer. Every stone, every speck of dirt or dried branch on that hill was infused with the same vitality as the grass, or the dog, or even my thoughts. Distance and time seemed to disintegrate while I looked out into an Entity which could only be described as ever-changing and infinite.

Could this be God?

Whatever it was, it had banished the darkness I had been feeling as surely as if it had never been there. *Hope!* There was more to existence than I had ever imagined.

Ecstatic, I turned to Dad who was still busy tossing the stick for Judy to fetch: “Dad, I’ve ... got something, something real. I don’t know how to say it. It’s ... fantastic.”

“Yeah, sure. Very good.”

He didn’t understand. How could he? I had no words to describe what I was feeling.

We walked back in silence. I breathed as deeply as I could. The air had never before tasted so beautiful.

### A decision

Months later. The night was starless, moonless, dark. In a silence covered by wind, Dad and I, and Judy, took a walk along the empty side streets, our path shrouded by whispering trees, which were heavy with leaves. Even Judy seemed sunk in contemplation—quiet except for the velvet rhythm of her foot pads shuffling over the concrete.

“So, what are you thinking to do with your life, my boy?” Dad asked me, his voice blending with the wind.

I looked at him to make sure he wasn’t joking. No, surely not. He was staring straight ahead, noticeably anxious about what I might say. Our pace slowed.

“Well ... I’ve read a lot about spirituality, yoga, religion and self-realization.... I want to put it into practice. It’s been just books until now. I want ... I want ... to find the truth, you know.”

He turned and leaned towards me until his face was all that I could see. Then he lifted his eyebrows and said loudly, dramatically, “You mean GOD?”

For a brief moment, I lost my stride.

“Well ... yeah ... guess you might as well say I want to find God.”

Seconds passed. The dog’s leash tinkled against her tags. I

wondered if he thought me immature, arrogant, foolish or what.

When he answered, his voice sounded muffled as if it were coming from behind a wall. “Good.”

I was surprised.

“What? You think it’s good? Then why haven’t you done it? Why didn’t you try?”

His voice suddenly sounded older than I had ever heard it. “I don’t have the guts.”

I am sure Dad had no idea how his honest answer affected me, and what a strong sense of determination it created in me.

# CHAPTER 2

## What a Fool I’ve Been

*(In September 1969, I entered Reed College, in Portland, Oregon, a school with a reputation for attracting students who were somehow a bit ‘different’: individualistic, alternative, anarchistic, progressive.)*

### Shifting gears

Portland. One evening, a friend invited me to attend a lecture by Richard Alpert, otherwise known as Baba Ram Dass. He had been a professor at Harvard who was kicked out for experimenting with LSD and other psychedelics, then went to India and became a yogi. It did not especially interest me so I declined the invitation.

I began the evening with some other friends. As we were walking across the campus, we saw a large crowd gathered in the dining hall. Curious, we wandered in the backdoor. About two hundred students sat in chairs facing a makeshift stage at the front of the hall. On the stage was a man dressed completely in white and sitting cross-legged. Except for his voice, the room was absolutely silent.

His voice captivated me. I walked forward and leaned

against a pillar, watching him. My mind became still; nothing remained except his voice. I was astonished at this sudden shift in my consciousness. His lecture was full of stories about centering oneself upon the present, instead of worrying about the past or future. Only when he mentioned his guru in India did I realize that he was Baba Ram Dass.

I slowly moved forward, experiencing an irresistible attraction. The pull was so strong that I eventually found myself on the stage, sitting next to him. Rather than objecting to my impudence, he seemed to welcome me. Soon I forgot about the audience. It was only he and I sitting there. As he continued to talk, I gradually became completely calm, calmer than I had ever been.

The time passed without my noticing it. He stood up. The program was finished. He began walking. Still entranced, and hardly noticing the other students, I walked with Ram Dass out of the door, and up to a car. He turned, smiled at me, and drove away. At that moment, I had a queer feeling that my life was changed forever.

I walked back into the hall in a daze. On the stage, one of the students was making an announcement, “This talk was recorded and we will refer to it for making a book. Those who are interested should fill in one of these cards, and you will receive a free copy.” I filled in a card.[^1]

### A beginning

Since the experience two weeks previously, I had felt a continuous desire to find a spiritual master, and learn meditation. I had not yet found a master, but at least on this day I had my chance to learn meditation.

***

[^1]: Some months later I did receive that promised free copy. Later this book, entitled *Be Here Now*, became famous among spiritual seekers. It continues to sell well even today, in the twenty-first century, and ranks among the five all-time best-selling spiritual books. The copy I received was so original that it was bound not with glue, but with spiral plastic.

Instructors from a renowned spiritual school came for the first time to Reed College. They offered to teach meditation for a steep price. I thought it improper that they charged money for spiritual food, and I convinced them to teach me for free. The technique was essentially the repetition of a mantra—a Sanskrit word used as an object for concentration. I asked them the meaning of my mantra and was told there was none.

Two months later, in 1970, I still had no special or even interesting experience with my meditation, so I stopped it.[^2]

Then, three months later, I thought that something was better than nothing. Even though I did not feel inspired with meditation, I resumed it. I decided to continue until I could find a superior teacher or system.

### No outside

A forest in northern California. Geographically, I hardly knew where I was. It did not matter. I set up my tent in a densely forested valley bordered on all sides by mountains. I had not come here to enjoy nature (though it was enjoyable), to relax or to escape. My sole purpose was to spend a few days concentrating with undivided attention on attaining at least an ounce of spiritual revelation.

I passed all my time that day meditating, except for a few short breaks to read the biography of an Indian yogi, for some light food, and a little walking.

Two days later, when I awoke in the morning, my mood was already exalted. I bathed in the cold water of the river running through this valley, then sat on my blanket for meditation. The sun, still behind the mountains, reflected a soft red light off the leaves of the trees surrounding me. I closed my eyes.

Ever so slowly, my mind dipped into a dimension I had

[^2]: Much later, I understood this technique to be a relaxation technique. It was meditation in name only. Proper meditation not only calms the mind, but also contemplates the infinite.

never known before. I felt like I was falling continuously from a great height. Every few moments, I found myself uncontrollably sucking in a strong breath—as if I was shocked again and again by something suddenly appearing without warning.

After thirty minutes or an hour, it stopped. I became very calm. When I opened my eyes, the world was somehow different. It was the same forest, but it was much closer to me. I felt like I was touching every object within sight. Something was happening. Intent not to lose the chance, I stared at this scene, not moving a muscle.

A few thoughts fluttered by. A bird chirped intermittently. The leaves glittered. The grass swayed. I was alone, and yet not alone. Someone was there. Who was it?

My breath slowed, lengthened. I watched and listened. Was it God or what? Or was I going loony? I didn’t care. Someone was definitely here or coming. I had to pierce this mystery.

Suddenly I knew. My eyes widened in astonishment. My mouth fell open. Of course! Why had I never seen it before? It was me! On all sides—me. The trees were me. The mountains were me. The “bird” chirped when I wanted because that sound was my own thought. The blades of grass waved slowly back and forth exactly according to my desire because the whole scene was inside my mind. It was my movie. I looked down. Even my body was just another part of the picture. Just like a dream. But this time I was intensely awake.

Think of it! All my life I had been worried about the people in this world, never realizing they were nothing other than my own mind. Absolutely nothing here except my mind—my own colorful, vibrant thoughts in the form of bushes, insects, light, clouds—everything.

At last my mind spoke: *What a fool I’ve been, worrying and fretting!* There was nothing to harm me and nowhere to run to. Every single minutest part was me. I had made up this tense drama. There were no separate people, no separate houses, no

teachers, no enemies, no problems. They were all my own creation. The whole universe was laughing.

The whole universe? I had thought it so vast, immeasurable. Yet it was only a thought: changing its colors and shapes and sounds and feelings precisely as I wanted.

That’s it! As I wanted. That which caused this scene to change was my desire. There were three things: my consciousness, my desire and the expression of my desire. Nothing else.

No. It was not even that complex. Whatever I saw was my desire itself; there was no internal or external. Whatever I desired, I immediately experienced because my desire was my thought which was my world. So there were only two things: my consciousness and its momentary ever-changing whim or form.

How curious! How funny!

A desire arose: *I must never come down from this state.* I quickly wrote about my realization in a notebook.

But wait. Again I was playing the fool. How quickly I forgot. There was nothing outside of me—so how could I ever “come down”? There was no down or up, except in my imagination. Happy or sad—what the hell? Just me, everywhere that I looked, sometimes sparkling, sometimes laughing.

Another thought came: *This is what the yogis call bliss. The One-ness. The truth. What has always been and always will be. Always, because time is also my creation. Past, future—what a scam. What a clever trick I’ve played on myself. Just one entity and nothing else. No way to go outside of it. I cannot die because “I” was never born. I’ve always just been changing form.*

How could I explain this to anyone else? To my friends or my family or my teachers in Portland? What a moron I was! They were all just my thoughts. Even the skyscrapers and the tense exams and the baseball I dropped and the car from which

I fell when I was four years old and China and the President and the TV. What a joke! Perfect, absolute, without a loophole.

And “God”. That thought, too, was my own creation. Not a bad thought, that one. Because God was all this—this game. *Yes. I am God. But not only this little body or these passing thoughts. No no. Everything is me, is God. “God”, “Guru”—I made them up just to suit my story.*

Ah, nothing to worry about and nowhere to go. Ah ... ahh ... ahhhhh....

Later. Very little light left to write by. The sun was setting. I was shocked: so much “time” had passed! Insects were humming but they did not sound so friendly any more. It was getting cold, very cold. Well, yes, it’s all me, but no need to antagonize myself.

I started to shake nervously. Pulling myself together, I arranged my blanket in the tent as cold air pushed against my neck and up my shirt sleeves and pant-legs.

Yes, it’s all me but it’s cold. And I was not feeling so great. Well, it did not matter. I would tolerate it and go on until—until what? Hmm. It’s all me alright, but where was I going? I still hadn’t answered that. And what, by God! What will I do now?

It was getting so cold so fast that I wondered whether I would be able to sleep at all.

### CHAPTER 3

![A person with a backpack hitchhiking on a road holding a "VANCOUVER" sign as a car approaches.](illustration)

# *Just Love Me*

**Seven teachings**

Portland, 1971. I wrote some doggerel, trying to catch the nitty-gritty of my experience over the last two years, during which I passed through six teachers and groups. At the time of writing I was with the seventh group.[^1] I know that the reader will excuse my critical reference to other groups at the time: each person has his own spiritual path to take and the doggerel demonstrates in mock-heroic form how I made my choice. It is reproduced below, unedited, unprocessed, consisting of a youth’s maybe rather impolitic reflections:

> I met a religious group
> &emsp;hitch-hiking through the state of Unease
> (Which group matters little—

[^1]: After the experience I had in 1969, I could not help but feel more and more the presence of an infinite Being. I did not like the word “God” because of the hundreds of connotations and dogmas it inferred. But there was no way past the limitation of words. The development of my feeling for God was too gradual to describe, so please excuse the gap in my explanation.

> &emsp;pick any one, reader, as you please.)
> Their perfect scripture
> &emsp;preached love of God and man,
> and unswerving devotion
> &emsp;to its holy Plan.
> Although their Hero was long dead,
> &emsp;they tried to live by what He’d said:
> “Be saved, follow my words.
> &emsp;Sinner or thief,
> ye shall go to Heaven.
> &emsp;Just hold My belief.”
>
> I liked
> &emsp;their service programs
> &emsp;&emsp;for the sick and hungry,
> &emsp;&emsp;&emsp;their will to suffer,
> &emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;and their monastery.
>
> But
> &emsp;The goal of heaven
> &emsp;&emsp;was finally just an escape.
> &emsp;The poor first must pray,
> &emsp;&emsp;or don’t get even a grape.
> &emsp;Superiority to other groups
> &emsp;&emsp;made no real sense.
> &emsp;Before words of criticism
> &emsp;&emsp;they built a fence.
>
> Moving on…
>
> I experienced
> &emsp;group after group nonstop.
> Probing, searching,
> &emsp;not just to window-shop.
> Though the teachings turned progressively better,

each one contained some dogmatic fetter.
You must excuse me
if I was wrong,
I had no choice
but to move along.

The second group
taught concentrated calm.
They said no need
of any other balm.

The third aimed to kill desire,
strengthening harmony and will-fire.
Meditating on Nothing’s power,
they forgot the Devotional Flower.

The fourth was joyous,
full of life and mystical.
But too much ritual
and doctrine: theistical.

The fifth was potent
for one’s mind and physique.
Though Divine love and
selfless service were weak.

The sixth seemed devotional
in dance, work, prayer and food.
But as the goal was paradise,
the teaching itself was crude.

The seventh is healthy and loving.
They speak without prejudice.
Their meditation is subtle,
yet I feel something amiss.

Within my heart I speak to Him,
“God, I don’t know where to swim.
Though I’m not content
(yes, something’s absent),
if You show no other road,
the seventh will be my abode.”
In humility I take this stand.
At last perhaps He’ll give His hand.

### Spiritual communes

I had just completed one month visiting several spiritual communes in Canada and the USA, and I had decided that the best place for me would be the one in California. Honestly, I was not completely satisfied with my present technique but there seemed to be no better alternative. It was up to God. I thought, *If He did not give me anything better it was not my fault. Anyhow, the place in California should be OK. I would be able to manage my finances through a little farming and housekeeping. And there would be plenty of time to meditate.* So I decided to leave for California the next day.

### Last on the list

Before leaving Portland, I attended the public lecture of an Indian monk of the spiritual movement, Ananda Marga. He was called *Dada*.^2

Because it was his first time out of India, his accent was so thick that I hardly understood a full sentence of his speech.

---

^2 *Ananda* is Sanskrit for bliss, and *Marga* means path. So *Ananda Marga* means “the path of bliss”. The meditation teachers who are men are called *Dada*, and the ladies are called *Didi*. *Dada* means “elder brother”, and *Didi* means “elder sister”. Except for some elderly Dadas and Didis who are family-people, all are renunciate monks and nuns. Besides teaching meditation, they remain busy organizing social service programs. Later I came to know that this particular monk was Dada Yatishvarananda.

Moreover, I was not impressed with the Margiis, so I was not attracted to join the group. Nevertheless, I wanted to meet this saint, just to tell him my plan. Unfortunately, my name was put at the bottom of the waiting list because I had no interest to learn meditation, only to talk. Seventy people signed up to learn, so I had to wait two days just to see him.

### Initiation: clash and cohesion

This morning, at last, I walked into Dada’s little room in the yoga house. He prepared to teach me.

“Excuse me, Dadaji,” I said. “I did not come for initiation.”

“Then what do you want?” he said.

“I only want your opinion regarding my plan.” I took a couple of minutes to explain my spiritual search and decision to go to the California commune.

“So what do you think?” I asked.

“It’s okay,” he said.

I sat quietly, waiting, but he added nothing more. Had I killed my time waiting to meet this man?

“Ah.... I don’t know how to say this, Dadaji, but you see, I waited two days to meet you. Is it possible that you could add something more?”

“Are you sure you want me to speak more?” he said. “You may not like what I have to say.”

“Please, please, go ahead.”

He then gave me a ten-minute lecture about the difficulty of performing meditation twenty-four hours a day, that I was living in this world and enjoying the benefits of others’ labor, but was not thinking to contribute anything to society, except for some vague hope that my meditation itself would add to the world’s positivity. Why did I not consider doing social work during the hours when I would not be doing meditation? Could I not see that commune life escaped the urgent needs of the real world?

When he finished, I said, “Now let me see if I got you

straight. In short, are you saying that I should leave my present practice and, instead, join Ananda Marga?”

“Yes.”

“Wow, that’s not what I expected! I have to go and think about it for a while.”

I left him, and went to the meditation room. I sat down, determined to get an answer to his proposal.

I did deep and long meditation as best as I knew, but no answer came.

I asked God, but no answer came.

I asked my present guru, and then I asked the guru of Ananda Marga for an answer—but nothing.

Several hours passed. Due to my inclination for social work, I felt some regard for Ananda Marga, but otherwise nothing was clear. At last, still uncertain, I stood up.

My concentration had been so intense that I had not noticed that lunch time had come and gone. I walked onto the lawn, where Dadaji was sitting with a large group of new people.

“In the future, it will be you, the young people of today, who will be the leaders. You carry an awesome responsibility because our world needs dramatic changes if it is to survive. Who knows, maybe he will be the future American president.” As he said this last sentence, he pointed at me.

In that moment my mind flew out of control; I no longer knew what I was thinking. My ears pounded, and my head swam. Dadaji continued to speak, but I understood nothing more.

After some time, when my head cleared, I took a decision—I must not be selfish. I should not only perform meditation, but also do social work. I should join Ananda Marga.

Within an hour I was again sitting with Dadaji, taking the initiation. I then performed my new meditation and felt very pleased.

A friend of mine, Chris, had also learnt the same

meditation. Now it was time for him to leave Portland, and return to his university in Eugene. As I stood outside waiting for him, my brief euphoria abruptly ended and turned into a confused depression.

When he saw my dark face, he asked, “What’s the matter? Only moments ago you were the happiest guy in the world.”

“What have I done?” I said. “For years now I’ve been switching from path to path, and guru to guru. I’m like a leaf floating wherever the wind blows. Will this never finish? Why did I do this? Am I really following God’s will, or just my own ego? I’m so mixed up I can’t stand it.” I started weeping.

He put his arm around my shoulders, and together we walked toward the bus station. I went on speaking in such a pitiful manner that Chris also began crying. When we reached the bus, I said to him, “Oh, what am I going to do? I’m lost, lost.”

“I don’t know,” he said. “I don’t know anything.”

As the bus drove away, he waved from the window. Our two faces were bathed in tears.

Under the early evening darkness, I slowly walked toward the yoga house. I had left all my gear there, having no other place to stay and no idea where else to go. A drizzle was falling, adding to my sorrow.

Suddenly, like a bolt of lightning, my mind lit up with an idea: “God has no interest to trick anyone.”

“Of course! Why am I worried?” I thought. Though the sun was gone, it seemed like day time. “The reason I entered Ananda Marga was to sacrifice myself in helping others. What more can He expect from me? Nothing. Surely, if I do my best, it is earthly perfection in God’s eyes.”

One moment I had been in hell and the next moment I was flying back up to some blissful heaven. I had no explanation for it but I knew, somewhere deep inside, that the changes I had just gone through were destined to affect my thinking and my outlook for the rest of my life. All I could do was my best.

The rest was up to Him.

My step was light, as I headed toward the yoga house, full of a new hope.

From the moment when I again walked into the house, I began working full-time for Ananda Marga.

### Baba’s status

Days later I was still feeling subconsciously slightly troubled about having left my previous guru for the Ananda Marga guru: Shrii Shrii Anandamurti, also known as Baba.[^1] In the night I had a dream:

I was looking down a long stairway but could see only two or three steps. The rest were covered by mist. I hesitated, afraid to walk down into that cloud, but then anyway went ahead. Nothing was visible on either side. After stepping down into the mist, I saw a book on the stairway. It was a book I never saw before, of the group that I had left to join Ananda Marga. Opening it, I saw a photo of their guru. On the next page was a photo of the guru’s guru. Turning the pages, I saw photos of their ancient lineage of gurus.

Further downstairs, another book appeared. I walked down and picked it up. It was the book of Ananda Marga’s guru.

“For the first time I’ll see Baba’s photo!” I thought. I opened the book, full of curiosity. But instead of seeing a man’s image, my eyes were dazzled by a brilliant golden light streaming out of the page.

“He’s greater than any other,” I thought.

Then I woke up, feeling blissful.

The last vestige of uneasy feeling about having left my

[^1]: In India, many holy men are called *Baba*, meaning “beloved one” or “loving father”. Baba had also one more name: P.R. Sarkar, which was His legal name. Using this legal name, He wrote books on social and economic philosophy. Only spiritual philosophy was written under the name Shrii Shrii Anandamurti.

previous guru was gone.

### Freedom through giving

During a meeting of the yoga house’s residents, the finance secretary said, “The rent is due tomorrow, and we are $180 short. Does anyone have an idea what to do?”

Since I had exactly that amount in my Portland account, it seemed a cosmic sign, and a small test for me.

Before I could think twice, I quickly said, “To the cent there’s precisely $180 in my account. I’ll give it to you tomorrow.”

Everyone cheered, and the finance secretary said, “Baba solves our problems every single time. I always worry, but He always does it.”

Though I had more money back in Chicago, this donation, made fully according to capacity, left me totally free.

### Releasing selfishness

I completed my first week in Ananda Marga. At first, I had a little difficulty adjusting to the new meditation technique. My concentration was poor. A few days later, however, a fresh seed germinated. I learned a spiritual chant and dance called *kiirtan*, which made it easy to concentrate on God.[^4]

I saw that until now my purpose in being on the spiritual path—to realize Truth or God—had really been for the sake of

[^4]: *Kiirtan* is the practice of singing God’s name while dancing. In Ananda Marga, the chant is *“Baba Nam Kevalam”*. Here *Baba* means “dearest One, God, most Beloved, or divine Father”; *Nam* means “name”; *Kevalam* means “only”. While dancing, one holds the arms high, and steps from side to side, touching the big toe of one foot next to the heel of the other foot. Kiirtan is a purely devotional practice for the purpose of bringing one’s mind into a concentrated spiritual state before beginning silent meditation. Because it enhances the practice of meditation, it is best to dance kiirtan before every meditation whenever practical, whether alone or in a group. It is the single practice which provides the greatest assurance against developing superiority complex, inferiority complex, or other harmful complexes of ego.

myself. Plainly speaking, it had been selfish.

Kiirtan was a way out of that selfishness. It was a dance of surrender to God, a dance of giving Him myself. Singing of Him, dancing for Him—nothing for me.

During my first kiirtans I was sometimes self-conscious, thinking, “Am I doing it right? What will others think of me doing this dance?” But I soon overcame that and, after I sat down for meditation, the flow continued: for Him, not for me.

A big burden was being released, a burden I did not even know I carried. Whenever I did kiirtan nicely, selfish thinking stopped or almost stopped. But not by suppression. Where “He” was, automatically my “I” was not.

Who or what was He? I did not know. Occasionally, during kiirtan or meditation, I was afraid that I was losing myself to something unknown and I drew back. How silly! There was nothing to lose except my selfishness.

### Midnight message

This night I went to sleep at 11:00 in a second-story room of the yoga house. The other bed next to mine was empty.

About midnight, I was awakened by a voice. By the moonlight coming through the window, I could see a man lying on the other bed wearing white pajamas.

In a firm voice he said, “Get up and tell everyone that no one is to go to sleep without first doing their second meditation.”

Though surprised, I stood up unquestioningly and walked out of the room to the stairway. Looking down, I saw only one brother, Brian, sitting on the sofa.

“I don’t know quite what’s happening,” I mumbled down to Brian, “but, ah, there was a man on the other bed in my room, and, oh, pardon me, it’s all so strange, maybe it doesn’t make sense, but well, anyway, I’ll repeat what he said: ‘No one is to go to sleep until they’ve done their second meditation.’ That’s what he said. So excuse me, and good night.”

As I was talking, Brian was looking around, unable to find the speaker. In the end, as I turned toward my room, he glanced up and saw me.

When I entered my room, I looked at the second bed. Nobody. But a powerful oscillating energy was pervading the air, walls, floor, furniture, everything. It made me feel dizzy. It was as if the room had been mystically transported onto an ocean-going ship, which bobbed up and down as it moved over powerful waves. I lay down, bewildered, and merged into that vibration. Within no time I fell asleep.

In the morning, Brian came up to me and said, “That was incredible last night.”

“Last night? What?” I said. My mind was blank.

“You don’t remember? Really strange! I was sitting alone late last night, feeling exhausted and a bit sick, thinking, ‘I just can’t do my meditation tonight. Baba, don’t mind. I’ll go to sleep.’” As he said this, my memory clicked and it all flashed back.

“Yeah, I remember now!”

“Well, anyway, as I started to get up I heard your voice, but couldn’t understand where it was coming from. In fact, I thought it was Baba’s voice! And what He said through you made my whole body shake until I mentally threw myself at His feet. After that, when I looked up and finally saw you, well, I was shocked again. Of course I went to my room after that and did long meditation.”[^5]

Perhaps this experience deeply affected me because, after that, I never missed doing meditation twice a day. And some months later, I upped it to at least four times daily—permanently.

***

[^5]: In order to receive Ananda Marga initiation, two disciplines are required: meditation at least twice daily, and participation in the weekly group meditation. The student is free to experiment with many other practices and exercises, but the most crucial one is regular meditation.

### Clean-shaven

For the first time, I saw a photo of Baba. I was stunned. It was the same man who was in my room three nights before. I had always imagined that Baba would have a great beard and powerful eyes. But the man who had been in my room, and who I now realized was Baba Himself, was clean-shaven, wore glasses, and appeared more like a gentleman than a yoga master. That He was a yoga master, I was no longer in doubt, and I saw that He was more powerful than I could ever have imagined.[^6]

### A challenge

About six hundred Margiis from all over America were attending the retreat in Oklahoma. I participated in a meeting with seventy of them who wanted to work as full-time volunteers. I volunteered to go to Mississippi, which was one of the few states yet unstaffed. Mississippi was infamous for being the state with great racial hatred between blacks and whites. It promised to be an interesting challenge to introduce universalism to such people.

I arrived in Jackson, Mississippi with one other new volunteer. In the night we slept peacefully under a bush.

We did not have a single friend, reference or contact.

Because there was a shortage of houses, the real estate companies all said that it took from three months to one year on the waiting list before one could rent a place. Nevertheless, we found an agency that made an exception for us.

It was a small house, but it would keep the rain out better than a bush.

***

[^6]: Though Baba recommended that men should keep at least two-inch beards so that the normal temperature of the related glands is maintained, He nevertheless shaved. Under the demand of social conditions, a man may have to shave. Baba’s appearance demonstrated that there is no spiritual difference between renunciates and family people. The same potential enlightenment is everyone’s birthright, even for those who are compelled to moderately compromise their lifestyles.

### Speaking through a donkey’s mouth

I gave my first lecture on yoga at the university. Since nothing was planned, I completely depended on Baba to guide me.

There were forty students plus a few teachers—all from the Department of Psychology. After they introduced me, I stood dumb before them. Ten seconds, twenty seconds, passed. I began to swear, and mentally screamed at Baba, “Why aren’t you giving me any ideas?”

Baffled at His unexpected non-cooperation, I started speaking something, anything. As a few awkward words emerged in a slow, nervous stutter, I thought, “Baba, help me!” Suddenly an intangible wave rolled over me. The fear evaporated, and words began to flow. I spoke for one hour. At the end of it, everyone applauded enthusiastically.

While we were walking back to our flat, David, the other volunteer, said, “Wow, that was perfect! They all seemed totally absorbed in your words. And so amazed, they couldn’t ask a single question after your speech! Afterwards I heard several people saying that they felt you spoke straight to their inner needs. Where did you get such ideas?”

Still excited from the experience, I said, “Before I try to answer your question, can you answer one of mine? What did I talk about? I don’t remember a single sentence.”

### Graced by disgrace

One of the new Margiis asked me if I knew that some of the neighbors were disturbed by our yoga house, and had complained to our real estate agent.

“No,” I said. “What’s their grievance?”

“They said it’s disgraceful that both blacks and whites are coming into this house together.”

I laughed, and said, “We’re already having a good effect on the neighborhood.”

### Just love me

A friend invited me to a séance conducted by a lady who claimed to be a spirit medium. At the séance, she entered into a trance which enabled her to “channel the spirit” by automatic writing. She instructed me to mentally ask a question without speaking it aloud. Though I believed that such channels are almost always just fooling themselves, still I could admit that a “medium” might be in touch with the unconscious mind, and thus might reflect otherwise unrevealed information.

I thought, “What should I do about the trouble I’m getting every time after meditation?” For some weeks, I had felt terribly exhausted at the end of meditation, and usually had to rest a few minutes afterwards.

The lady’s hand wrote, “Do not worry. It is not bad. It will end in ten days.”

Ten days later, the problem of exhaustion seemed to continue unabated, though I had not given a single thought to the medium’s message.

I went to the University of Mississippi in Oxford to begin a three-day lecture program. Before going to deliver my speech, I did meditation. As usual, I became sleepy during meditation. But this time the fatigue had become so great that I discovered myself sleeping on my back without even intending to lie down.

Angry with myself, I sat up to resume meditation. After a short time, I again fell asleep and had a powerful dream:

I was in India with a small group of American Margiis. We were sitting in an auditorium together with hundreds of other Margiis. Baba was on the stage, speaking. One by one, He called our names, asking each of the Americans to come on stage to be welcomed and embraced.

He called every name but mine, so I became frustrated. Then Baba walked off the stage and came over to me. He raised his foot and slowly pushed the sole of His shoe toward

my face. As I watched, terrified, Baba’s form changed: He became the most abominable insect that I could imagine—complete with claws and fangs.

I wanted to run away, but froze in fear. I tried to scream, but nothing came out. Then I heard a voice coming from nowhere: *“Just love Me.”*

My fear broke a bit as I wondered about the voice. Again it came, “Just love Me.” I followed its command, and tried to love Baba in the form of that hideous insect. Gradually, the insect disappeared and was replaced by a faint white light. My love grew deeper, and the light became bright and warm.

For a moment my love subsided, and the black bug began to appear again. This time, however, I felt no loathing, and easily rekindled my love. The light returned, far brighter than the sun, though it did not hurt my eyes. All my anxieties drowned in endless bliss.

I awoke and thought, “I must never forget this lesson. Everyone and everything is His manifestation. Even the worst and vilest of forms is Him.”

I felt that this dream had released some old and deep tension in me. The problem of exhaustion after meditation had disappeared—exactly ten days after the medium gave her message.

# CHAPTER 4

> [Illustration: A group of people sitting on the floor in meditation and discussion.]

## *You Have To Work For Your Realization*

### The plan to leave Ananda Marga

Jackson, 1972. I had decided to leave Ananda Marga. Why? To answer this, I put forward another question: Is it correct to meditate on the idea, “I am one with God”? Day by day I was convincing myself of this statement, though honestly I felt I knew nothing with any certainty about life. My purpose in following a spiritual path was to go beyond my limited beliefs and realize the truth. Whether or not the idea “I am one with God” was right or wrong, I did not know. Maybe this idea also was just a belief.

I decided I should go to an uninhabited place, leave all my worldly possessions behind, and throw myself open to Being. Before joining Ananda Marga, I did this sort of exercise several times. But this time it would not be for a few days only. It would be without any time limit. Why should I trap myself with any kind of dogma?

Unfortunately, I was bound to stay in Jackson three more weeks because Dada Yatishvarananda was coming, and I was responsible for setting up his programs. As soon as his visit was finished, I would leave.

Three weeks later.... Over the last ten days, I did not do Ananda Marga meditation. I simply sat four times daily, trying to think of nothing.

When he arrived, Dada Yatishvarananda said, “Your face is drained of light. What’s troubling you?”

“Dadaji, I want to leave my post just after your visit is finished. To continue would be hypocrisy.” I explained my dilemma, and why I had to go.

He tried to help me, but nothing he suggested did any good. Finally he said, “We will do all of our meditations together for three days, and your questions will be answered.” I doubted it.

One hundred and fifty people came to the lecture that night—a grand success. Was it possible that I was efficient, not despite my non-interest but because of it, because of the absence of anxious expectation? During the past three weeks, though I had no personal interest in the work, I did it efficiently.

Two days later—and it was Dadaji’s final day here. I was so busy that I had no time to worry about whether my decision was correct or incorrect.
