He started weeping and shaking furiously. I put my hand on his shoulder to try to comfort him.

“Baba ... is ... gone.”

“What! What are you talking about?” I said.

His crying and shaking increased.

“Did you have a bad dream or something?”

“No. No. Oh, Baba. First Didi Hemavati called me from Korea. Now someone called from Calcutta. It’s true. Baba’s left His body.”

“It’s impossible! I can’t believe it, and I don’t accept it.”

“Oh, I don’t know what to do or what to think,” he said.

“No, this is ridiculous. Baba couldn’t have died. He said He would stay until His work was done. It must be something else. I am going to call Calcutta.”

After a few minutes, I was able to get through. Dada Bhaveshananda answered.

“What happened?” I said.

His voice was sober, slow and somewhat shaking, “It seems ... Baba left.”

“What do you mean ‘seems’?”

“The life force is gone for some hours now. No pulse. Nothing.”

“Perhaps He’s in *samadhi* or some special state. Maybe He will come out of it after some time.”

“Well, we hope so. But ... I don’t think so.”

I tried to think of something else to say, but my mind went blank. I passed the phone to Artapremanandaji, and he spoke briefly in Hindi. Then he hung up. He turned to me, saying, “What should we do?”

“Well, I simply don’t believe Baba’s died,” I said, almost calmly. “But in any case, something extraordinary’s happened, so I want to go to India immediately.”

“Yes, I’ll also go.”

“Now we should tell the other Dadas.”

“Oh, I can’t talk anymore.”

“It’s okay. I’ll tell them.”

I woke up the others and brought them into the room. I felt very cold, but otherwise almost normal. After speaking a few words of introduction to prepare their minds, I said, “They’re all telling that Baba has died.”

No one said a word. But all four of them stared at me eerily. Their faces all wore strange expressions, and they edged closer toward me.

“There’s no explanation,” I said. “And personally, I just don’t believe it.”

They could hardly speak. I could not tell why, but it seemed that they immediately accepted it as true. Without saying a word, Moksanandaji got up, took his jacket, and walked out of the house. The others wandered aimlessly or sat and stared at the walls.

I was sure there was more to the story. Unworried, I took a shower, entered the meditation room, and sang kiirtan as if it were an ordinary day. No one joined me.

At 9:00 I phoned our travel agent and booked tickets for Artapremanandaji and myself. By 10:00 we were out of the door and on our way to the airport.

Sitting in the plane, I was still feeling relatively normal when Artapremanandaji turned to me and said, “I just

remembered something Bhaveshananda told me which I didn’t tell you. They’ve already put Baba’s body on the ice.”

His words hit me like a sledgehammer. “What? But then ... He must really be ... Oh, God, no! Oh, no! Then He’s really dead!”

I turned my face away. I cannot clearly remember what happened at this point, except that I went mad. A million ideas and visions passed through me. I thought of every possibility of what it meant for Baba and for me. I wept at my loss, wept at the world’s loss, worried for Baba, blamed Him, felt as lonely as a person floating on a piece of driftwood in the middle of an empty ocean, burned in my physical heart, felt guilty, felt rejected, felt insane. I even thought of leaving Ananda Marga. Why should I stay without Baba? Our mission was not yet finished, and we would never be able to complete it without Him.

After about an hour, I regained my composure. It was just a test. He knew what He was doing, as He had always known. There must be a great purpose behind it. Though I never imagined that He would do such a thing, I had to accept it. Though it was intolerable, I had to tolerate it. I would have to go ahead, and I steeled my mind for the task. I forced it to be positive, if not happy.

Throughout this time I had not spoken to Artapremanandaji. At this point I turned toward him, saying, “I’m okay now.” He nodded his head, though he had no idea what I had just passed through.

When we reached Bangkok, we called Japan. Only a few minutes before, Moksanandaji had returned. After receiving the news, he had strayed alone through the streets of Tokyo for fifteen hours.

### He remains active without His physical body

I received an emergency visa from the Indian embassy in Thailand. After discussing the matter with a few workers, it was decided that all Didis and Dadas could come. We phoned

Japan, Taiwan, Korea and Hong Kong.

At 2:00 a.m., while standing in the line for the immigration check in Delhi, I befriended a Japanese tourist and translated all the procedures for him.

Just after receiving my entry stamp, an elderly official in civil clothes directed me to the side of the room.

“You’ve come for the funeral, haven’t you?” he said.

“What funeral?”

“You know very well. The Anand Marg funeral.”

“I’m sorry, I don’t know what you’re talking about.”

“Listen. I’m an old man. I’ve seen you in Calcutta. I know you’re a Margii.”

“Sir, I’ve never been to Calcutta.”

“I don’t want to disturb your program. If you admit the truth to me, I will allow you to go ahead for the funeral. Why should I trouble you at a moment like this? But if you persist in this drama, I will have you deported. Now, please confirm that you are an Anand Margii.”

“A what? Look, it’s very late. I’m tired, and I don’t know what you’re talking about.”

He turned toward another official and directed the Japanese man to be pulled out of the line, suspecting him also to be a Margii. I would have laughed internally, but I was not in great humor. For God’s sake, I was enough troubled, and now on top of it, I was going to get deported. Surely they would find out the truth because my suitcase contained my orange uniforms, and Ananda Marga books and documents. I had never before been stopped like this, except in Calcutta, so I had not taken any precautions.

He asked questions for another half hour. I desperately clung to my story that I did not know Ananda Marga, and was following nobody and nothing except my own nose. He did not believe a single word. Meanwhile, the Japanese fellow was questioned by someone else.

At last, the old man turned to an assistant, saying, “Go check his luggage for any Anand Marg related items.”

As I hopelessly handed over the luggage key, I thought, *Baba, if You get me out of this one, then I’ll accept You’re still active, even without Your physical body.*

About ten minutes later, the assistant returned. He shook his head.

“I can’t believe it,” said the officer in Hindi. “Are you sure there’s nothing?”

“Nothing,” said the assistant, shrugging his shoulders.

The officer scowled. He stared into my eyes. I looked back nonchalantly, hardly believing my luck. “I’m sure you’re a Margii,” he said. “But ... you’re free to go now.”

“Thanks a lot,” I grunted. Then I thought, *Baba, You’re still with me! How did You manage it?*

I picked up my luggage, and walked to the green channel.

“Do you have anything to declare?” asked a customs official.

“Only a small cheap camera,” I said.

“Show it to me,” he said.

Opening my suitcase, I shuffled my orange clothes and other possessions around. The camera was not there, and neither was a box of empty cassette tapes. So that was it! The assistant knew I would never expose his theft.

“Sorry,” I said. “Forgot I left it in Thailand.”

As the official waved me through, I looked back and saw the poor Japanese man sweating under the old man’s investigation.

### Losing my center

Calcutta. Thousands of Margiis were present, eyes glazed or filled with tears.

Because I had already adjusted myself during the plane flight with Artapremanandaji, I felt calm. I stood in a long line waiting to enter Baba’s house to see His body. Next to me stood Chintamani, a Margii from Norway. He was particularly lucky, even inspired. After not going to India for about ten

years, something had pushed him to come here one month ago. He was blessed to experience Baba in the last weeks of His life.

We were calmly talking together when, suddenly, unexpectedly, a surge of feeling rose from my chest and, before I knew what I was doing, I was sobbing. As my knees gave way, Chintamani held me up.

“It’s okay, Dadaji, it’s okay,” he said.

Now I had lost my center. Nothing could console me. I dragged myself toward the house, crying continuously.

When I entered the door, I saw Him. His face was peaceful, beautiful. Somehow immediately I felt alright. He knew what He was doing. I accepted it fully, and that was that.

After leaving the room, I went to the back of the line. I waited, and again entered; again stared at Him. Again and again I made the circuit. It was my last chance to see Him, so why should I do anything else?

A few hours later, I thought, “Baba, please arrange it so that I can just stay in the room with You, without having to go out.”

A few minutes later, Dada Pranavananda approached me and asked me if I wanted to run the video camera inside Baba’s house.

I accepted, knowing that while it would not be enjoyable, it was a way to keep busy, and remain inside Baba’s room at the same time. Of course I thanked Baba for the arrangement.

I filmed thousands of Margiis as they shuffled around the body. Each face was unique, but each was filled with anguish. Occasionally, someone collapsed, screaming in agony. Some workers and Margiis who I highly respected, lost all control, and acted with unconscious abandon. As for me, at first I believed I had regained my composure. But again and again, I lost it, and wept bitterly.

I filmed many brothers and sisters that I had not seen in years. It was not the time, however, to say hello.

### Why and how He left

The Central workers called a meeting of overseas Margiis

and workers. Who was there who did not wonder why and how Baba had suddenly left us?

Baba’s personal assistant, Dada Keshavananda, told us about the days leading up to October 21:

“Since August, He was clearly in a hurry. He spent very little time alone, instead calling frequent group and individual meetings. Over the last weeks, many times in the wee hours of the morning, say 3:00 or 4:00, He woke me, and instructed me to call certain workers. The first time it happened I told Him that it was 3:00, and that they would surely be sleeping. He became angry, and said, ‘If those workers are not here within ten minutes, I shall never give them the program that’s in my mind.’ Of course I got them. We all became exhausted with His speed, but instead of slowing down, He accelerated. Sometimes we talked between ourselves, wondering at the cause of His haste. No one, however, came close to imagining that He was busy putting the final touches on His life’s work.

“Only one week before His departure, Baba gave kapalika initiation to the largest number of workers ever. He requested one hundred candidates. Because He had always been very selective in approving workers for this purpose, and was offered just a normal small number, it made Him angry, and He said, ‘I told you 100.’ We had no preparation for such a big number, and ultimately could only round up seventy-nine. He always rejected more applicants than He approved. But this time, without even asking any questions, He approved all. We were bewildered at His behavior.

“Immediately after the initiations, Baba became sick. Well, at least that was normal. After taking on many persons’ samskaras, He usually became sick. And this was by far the biggest number.

“On the last day, October 21st, His health actually improved. It was better than it had been in a long time. The morning was normal. He was working with the same tremendous speed that he had shown during these last months. Though late on the

previous night He had composed the final two *Prabhat Sangiitas* (numbers 5017 and 5018), He rose at 3:00 a.m. as usual to perform His spiritual practices. Later, He reviewed the work of various departments and gave instructions while shaving—even this was usual for His hectic schedule. He always told us, ‘You should not only be prepared to die while working, you should even work while dying.’ These words He clearly practiced.

“Shortly before 2:00 p.m., He said to me, ‘I want to think.’ As I shut the door, I thought, *He’s never said such a thing before.* After a few minutes, He called me. I believe it was during those minutes that He carefully reviewed all His planning, and confirmed that nothing remained to be done. He called one of the new avadhutas, and spent one hour alone with that Dada. That was the last work of Baba’s life— personal attention given to a young worker.

“After that, He said to me, ‘I want to rest now.’ These words also He had never before spoken. About five minutes later, He rang the call-bell and, when I came, He pointed at His chest, and said, ‘Heart.’

“I immediately ran for the doctors. They came quickly, and began massage. But as they felt for Baba’s pulse, their faces became ashen. When they said, ‘The life-force is gone,’ we could not believe it.”

Dada Mantreshvarananda, the Central Public Relations Secretary, said:

“Why did He leave so suddenly? I remember several years ago, Baba said, ‘When my work is done, I will leave immediately. I won’t remain even a single extra moment.’ He wrote hundreds of books, provided sufficient guidance in every realm of work and life, fully established the global structure and, most importantly, trained the workers. What more was there to do? The rest depends on us.

“Yes, it depends on us. About ten days before, I think it was

12th October, during the reporting session, Baba asked us three times, ‘Can I depend on you to carry on my mission?’ We repeatedly replied, ‘Yes, You can depend on us.’

“Many workers wondered what it meant, because the wording implied Baba was leaving us, though no one dared utter such a thought. Then He made us take the following oath:

“‘All my energy, all my mind, all my thoughts, all my deeds are to be goaded unto the path of the collective elevation of human society without neglecting other animate and inanimate objects right from this moment until the last point of my living on this earth.’

“The power was such that everyone became super-charged, and felt ready to do any work.

“A few days later, Baba asked me, ‘Mantreshvarananda, which *Prabhat Sangiita* do people generally like?’ I mentioned a few songs, then I added more, and still I couldn’t decide, and added even more. Baba said, ‘If I give you time, maybe you’ll list all the songs. But do people like, *Tumi esechile kaoke nabole nabolia cholegele?*’ (Meaning: You came without telling anyone, and You went back without telling anyone.) I said, ‘Yes, Baba, that is a very sweet song.’ I’ll remember forever how He smiled at that time.

“About the same time, while dictating a book to three of us—Vijayanandaji, the General Secretary and me—He said, ‘Do you know that all the doctors are experimenting on me? But they cannot cure me. And no one can cure me. It depends on my *samkalpa*—my vow of determination. If I think disease, disease comes. If I think cure, cure comes. And it completely depends on me what I should think.’

“The Calcutta Circle workers’ meeting finished on schedule on the 17th. But Baba did not give those workers permission to leave. This violated His long-standing system. The Delhi Sector meeting started, and also some global workers arrived. Only on the 21st, did we realize He had thus arranged for maximum workers to be here for His Great Departure.

“On the 20th, the night before His departure, He held a

meeting for Prout workers—both Margiis and Dadas. He questioned each Margii, one by one, taking their reports. Then He had us repeat the oath, the same one which we had pledged in the October 12th meeting. As far as my knowledge goes, this was the first and last time He had not only Dadas but also Margiis collectively making a promise. He then explained the situation of the whole world, describing how demonic people are making efforts to destroy the social and ecological orders, while good people are rapidly uniting together, preparing to fight those demons.

“Then He said, ‘Serpents are exhaling venom everywhere. It is the right moment, it is the proper moment, it is the most opportune moment. Now at this critical juncture, should we go on preaching the gospel of peace? No, no, no! So before my departure from this world, I have made the necessary preparation for the fight against these demons.’

“He added in verse-form:
> ‘*Serpents are exhaling venom everywhere.*
> *The sweet gospels of peace sound like empty mockery.*
> *That is why on the eve of my departure from this world,*
> *I send out a clarion call to all those in every house*
> *Who are preparing to fight against the demons in human form.*’

“We were all shocked. What is this? Baba is departing from this world? Then He added, ‘So said the great poet Rabindranath Tagore.’ We all breathed a sigh of relief, believing He had simply been quoting a poem. No one guessed that in truth this was His own message, and last message.[^4]

“After the reporting, Baba talked to me alone in His room.

[^4]: Baba’s exact Bengali while quoting Tagore’s poem was:
    Na’giniira’ ca’ridike pheliteche vis’a’kta vishva’s
    Sha’ntir lalita va’n’ii shona’ibe vyartha pariha’sa.
    Vida’y neba’r a’ge ta’i d’a’k diye ja’i
    Da’naver sa’the sam’gra’mer tare
    Prastiita hateche ya’ra’ ghare ghare.

He asked if everyone was inspired, which I confirmed. Then He asked me what they all thought about the poem. I said, ‘Baba, it is appropriate, and really it is happening in each Margii’s life.’ He nodded His head.

“One last thing I want to add. Two or three weeks ago, Baba said, ‘Many people may say, “Baba, I love you.” In truth, they may or may not love. But he who loves my ideology really loves me. Because I have merged myself with my ideology.’”

Himanshuji, Baba’s youngest brother, came to Calcutta, together with several other family members. He said:

“I was in His room on the last night, the 20th, with some of our family. While taking His meal, He spoke to us all, ‘Whenever the senior person of the family or the guru dies, the others must be very careful for one year, because many misfortunes may come.’

“I didn’t accept what He said at face-value, and said, ‘Why need it happen like that, Baba? It will all be due only to psychological reasons, which can be overcome.’

“‘No,’ He said. ‘There is no scientific explanation. You have simply got to accept it. Have you heard the phrase *Maha Guru Ni Pat*? It means the departure of the Great Guru. It means the nearest and most dear one leaves this world. Do you know what you should do in that case?’

“As we didn’t know, He continued, ‘For one year, one should not make any luxurious function, construct any special lighting or decoration, or arrange any party. Sorrow should be observed for one year. You must be very careful for one year.’

“I believed He was only speaking philosophy or theory. I never imagined we would have to apply His words from the very next day.

“Another point is that I used to inform Baba of the day I would return to my home and take permission from Him to leave. But this time, for the first time, He asked me when I was leaving. I was thinking of leaving after two days, that is Monday, but I thought He was asking because He wanted me

to stay much longer. Since I didn’t want to annoy Him, I couldn’t find any words to reply. Then in a loud voice, He said, ‘I’m simply asking if you’ll be here tomorrow, Sunday, or not.’ I said, ‘Yes, Baba, I’m leaving on Monday.’ He said, ‘I’m not asking about Monday, only Sunday.’

“At last He sang a folk song of Bihar to us. I laughed at this, thinking He was joking. He noticed my laugh, and said, ‘It’s not ordinary music. It’s a classical song.’ The song’s meaning was ‘If I will not be here any longer, my cart will still continue to move by the power of God.’”

Dada Vimalananda told us about an incident that explains, perhaps better than any, why He had to leave us:

“A few years ago, during a meeting of Proutists, one Margii asked, ‘Baba, when will Prout be established?’

“Baba replied, ‘When you are perched in a fruit tree, you can only eat the fruits on the tree, not the ones on the ground. When you stand on the ground, you can only eat the fruits which lie on the ground, not the ones hanging on the tree. Those who will get This (He pointed to Himself), will not get That (meaning Prout society). Which one do you want, This or That?’

“The Margiis protested, saying, ‘Oh, Baba, We want both. We want both Prout and You.’

“Baba said, ‘No, no. You can have only one or the other.’

“He didn’t solve all the problems before He left. He left the guidelines, showed some demonstrations, and provided the force to achieve the solutions. We have to solve the problems. We have to establish Prout.”

After this story, Vimalanandaji told us another one which explains something about the purpose of Baba’s life:

“In the early 1960’s, when Margiis could chat intimately with Baba, one Margii—perhaps Dasarath Dada, a family-

acharya—said, ‘Baba, You meditate every day even though You have no need to meditate. What is the object of Your meditation? And, Baba, if You don’t mind, can You tell us what is Your mantra?’

“Baba became quiet and then said, ‘I meditate on all of my children throughout the world.’

“The Margii added, ‘And Your mantra, Baba?’

“His mood shifted, and some profound emotion swept through His facial muscles as He said, ‘My mantra is *Manus’oham* (I am a human being). If I miss ideating on this mantra for even a single breath, My worldly existence becomes jeopardized.’

“With these words, Baba took a deep breath, and His body shivered. The Margiis grabbed His feet, rubbed them and, fearing He might in that very moment depart, they cried.”

### “Whatever happens I planned long ago”

In those days I listened to the stories of many Margiis and workers, about their dreams or visions that clearly foreshadowed Baba’s departure. I heard so many that it seemed like nearly everyone had some form of premonition.

One of those accounts came from Dada Aksayananda. It is a long story, but this one demonstrates better than any other how Baba pre-planned everything. Dada said:

“In 1979, Ananda Marga purchased a house in Lake Gardens, Calcutta, for our Marga Guru Quarters. I was then Baba’s second personal assistant, and frequently had the chance to talk with Him.

One day I was alone with Him and asked, ‘Baba, how would it be if we were to plant the grounds around the building and make a garden on the roof?’

“He replied, ‘You can do it. But if you plant around the house, the roots of the plants will damage the building’s foundation. If you make a roof garden, the roof will be spoiled by water draining out of the flower pots.’

“We didn’t talk for a few minutes. He seemed to be sleeping. Then I started thinking that we should get a much larger piece of land than this with a bigger house. Then I could make a big garden away from the building. There Baba could walk and enjoy the plants and the fresh air.

“Without opening His eyes, Baba suddenly asked, ‘Aksayananda, are you saying anything?’

“‘Nothing, Baba.’

“He opened His eyes. ‘No, no. You were telling me something.’

“I understood that Baba had been listening to my thoughts, so I replied, ‘Yes, Baba,’ and expressed my thoughts to Him. Baba only smiled and kept silent. He became reserved and silent for a few minutes. Then He closed His eyes for more than ten minutes. I wondered whether Baba was angry at me because at first I had told Him that I liked the new house, and then had started talking about getting another.

“Quite suddenly, Baba asked me to come near. I went close to His head, but Baba still did not open His eyes. I said, ‘Yes, Baba.’

“He opened His eyes with a loving smile, and placed both His palms on my cheeks.

“‘Yes, my son, what you thought may come true some day.’ He asked me to sit in meditation pose, close my eyes, and keep my mind concentrated in my sixth chakra. He then asked me what I saw.

“I told Baba that I saw a grand house surrounded by beautiful trees and many plants. He told me to go further and see more. Then I heard the sounds of hundreds of birds and saw that they were flying from tree to tree. Again Baba asked me to see more. I told Him that I saw Him walking under the trees on a narrow path. Some Dadas were with Him. Baba asked me to go towards the house.”

From this point in Dada’s story, it was basically just a conversation between the two of them:

“What are you seeing now?” Baba asked.

“I see that the building has two parts, one on the east and one on the west. Also there are a few underground rooms.”

“Go inside the western room. What do you see there?”

“Baba, there are rooms but no one is there.”

“Go downstairs to the underground room.”

“Baba, there’s not enough light to see clearly.”

“Go outside and look around.”

“Baba, I see the rooms in the eastern part of the building.”

“Go inside. Now tell me what you see.”

“Baba, there are many Dadas and Didis there.”

“Go further inside. Tell me what you see.”

“There is a door, but it’s closed.”

“Open it. What do you see?”

“I see You there, Baba, lying on a bed.”

“All right. Come out of the room and go upstairs. What do you see?”

“Baba, there is a big function going on with many people, and the room is beautifully decorated. Baba, You are sitting there with some other people.”

“Can you recognize them?”

“No, Baba. I have never seen them before.”

“Is anybody else there?”

“Yes. Two Dadas are doing something. They seem very busy.”

“And what else do you see?”

“Baba, I see a marriage going on.”

“Can you recognize the bride and groom?”

“No, Baba.”

“Now come downstairs. What do you see?”

“A fountain.”

“And now what do you see?”

“Baba, I see you going up in an elevator,” Dada answered with surprise.

“Go inside again. What do you see now?”

“Baba, all the Dadas and Didis are weeping.”

“Ask them why they are weeping.”

“Baba, I am asking them, but no one is replying. They just raise their heads and look at me. No one will answer.”

“Ask them again.”

“Baba, they are simply weeping, and not replying.”

“All right. Come out now. Do you recognize the location of the house?”

“It is somewhere in Calcutta, Baba, but not in Lake Gardens.”

Dada Aksayananda went on:

“Then the demonstration was over. Baba said, ‘Don’t tell anyone about this just now. Keep it to yourself for now.’

“Not long after, I was transferred to Kerala State. Four years later, Baba’s new residence and a very large headquarters for Ananda Marga was purchased in Tiljala, Calcutta.

“In 1983, I made my first visit there. There was no way for me to express my inner joy at finding that what Baba had shown me a few years before was now coming true.

“Years later, on the 11th September 1990, Baba came out of the hospital and at midnight He called for me. While I was massaging Him, He asked me to talk quietly about something so that He would feel sleepy.

“‘Baba, what you showed me in that demonstration in 1979 has come completely true now to the last detail.’

“I told Him that I had been wondering if the fountain would really be built, but it had come just as it was in the vision. Then the marriage was held of Baba’s adopted son, Kinshuk. Lastly the elevator was installed and reality was completely like my vision. I thought now I could tell everyone.

“‘You are forgetting one feature of the things I showed you. Try to recollect.’

“I tried and tried to remember, but I could not remember anything that was not already there in reality. I asked Baba to please tell me what it was that I had forgotten.

“He said, ‘When the time comes, you will remember everything. You see, whatever happens I planned it all long ago. Whenever I take determination for any purpose, that thing must happen and no one can stop it.’

“About thirty days later, Baba left His physical body.

“It was a bit after Baba’s passing, while talking about Him that I suddenly remembered the missing element in my vision to which Baba had referred. Yes, after Baba’s passing, all the Dadas and Didis wept in Baba’s quarters for two days. None of us could speak even if we tried. We just looked at each other and wept.”

### The orange fire

Since my arrival, the sky had been overcast. It drizzled continuously—adding to the gloomy feeling. I was told that the weather was fine until the moment Baba left on the 21st. The weather was still dismal.

About ten thousand Margiis were gathered on the front lawn in front of the Central Office. All of us either sat or stood around the great cement funeral pyre which had been constructed in the shape of a six-pointed star (as in our symbol). It would remain as a permanent reminder of this day. The crowd was abnormally restrained in their behavior. They sang non-stop *Prabhat Sangiit* and kiirtan.

At noon, the pall bearers appeared, carrying an open coffin in which Baba lay. Almost in one voice, thousands of persons let out a gasp, then a sigh. Though many wept quietly, the overall atmosphere was calm. According to Ananda Marga system, a short ceremony to console the bereaved was performed in which all participated. A final *Guru Puja* was sung, sung with a feeling never before known. Of course almost everyone was crying.

Purified butter was poured on His body, the fire lit, and thick, dark smoke rose. Exactly at that moment, two things happened. After six days of continuous clouds, the sky suddenly cleared sufficiently for the sun to shine down brightly, the rays illuminating only the cremation area. At the same time, a flock of birds dropped down from the clouds in a V-formation, executed a tight circle directly over His body, and flew off in steep ascent.

For three hours the orange fire burned. The crowd continued to sit and sing. During all that time, the sun shone brightly. At last, the fire was gone, and only some slight remains went on smoking. The sun again disappeared, the sky grew cloudy and somber, and gradually the Margiis dispersed.

Later that evening, I was sitting alone on the roof, thinking of nothing in particular. I felt freer than ever and full of love for Baba. For a moment, my mind looked at itself and wondered why it was feeling so peaceful. Immediately I knew: a previously unrecognized impediment in my relation with Baba had been burned.

I found that I had always harbored a fear, deep in my subconscious mind, that something in my guru was not perfect, that some sort of personal ambition or purpose might have affected Him. Though I had not been aware of this fear, it had nevertheless subtly affected me.

Now it was abundantly clear that He was a man who had not been guided by even the minutest ego. His death was the final proof. Though He had obviously planned His final moment in detail, He made no drama of it.

The manner of His departure was in perfect silent harmony with the message of His life.

# *Afterword*

It is twenty years since the previous edition, when I broke off my story just after Baba’s passing. Since then, of course, the drama has continued non-stop.

If my story were divided dramatically, my first years on the spiritual path and in Ananda Marga would be Act I, and the period I physically knew Baba would be Act II. With His physical departure, we are now in Act III. Act III is the most critical one, for it is in His absence that we will see the test of His teachings, and the love and Tantric power He exudes without the burden of a physical body.

A few years before He left, Baba mentioned in a workers’ meeting: “After my physical passing, Margiis and workers will be blessed with greater psychic and spiritual attention than I am presently able to provide. They will be very fortunate.” At that time, we gave little importance to His words, thinking He would remain with us for a long time.

It is still too early to know very much how Act III will unfold. A few highlights from the twenty years that have rapidly passed since He left, however, do give some indication of how it has been without the guru in physical form. To some extent they also bring the reader up to date.

And so my journey continues…

### Our case with Amnesty temporarily rests

In 1991, the woman from Amnesty International called me, saying, “I’m sorry, we have not yet received a reply from our London office concerning your case. I’m very concerned about the persecution in Ananda Nagar, so I intend to give London a big push. I thought to ask if you have any more information or documents you’d like to add.”

“I’m very thankful to you,” I said, “but there’s been a change. You see, in October, P.R. Sarkar, the propounder of Ananda Marga, passed away.”

“Oh, I’m very sorry to hear that.”

“No need to be sorry. There’s benevolent purpose in everything. Since then, the daily attacks at Ananda Nagar have ceased. It’s now completely peaceful, and we are free to engage in purely constructive work.”

“Oh, well, that’s a great relief to me.”

“I might add that the violence stopped, I guess, because the Communists believe Ananda Marga will crumble in the absence of Sarkar. But we always gain strength out of adverse conditions. The annual gathering in Ananda Nagar, which was concluded a few days ago, was even bigger than usual. Many thousands of people attended. A good number of them were new people who had not even seen Sarkar. So, I wouldn’t be surprised if, after watching our development for a few months or so, the Communists recommence their attacks against us.”

“Well, I sure hope not. In any case, I’ll keep your files safe. Let me know if the trouble starts up again.”

### Spiritual seeds planted long ago

In May 1994, I was in Komsomolsk, Russia. I found the people there receptive to spiritual development. In fact, some parts of the first *Vedas* were composed in central Russia 15,000 years ago.[^1] The Aryan people who migrated southeast mixed

[^1]: *Veda* means deep knowledge. The Vedic scriptures are the most ancient and voluminous of all the Indian spiritual literature. The Vedic writings are more philosophical and theoretical, while the Tantric texts are more practical.

their Vedic culture with the Tantric culture of the peoples of northeast India. Though the Aryans were an aggressive, warlike people, they were knowledgeable about spiritual philosophy.

The effect of those days seems to remain, because many Russians are extremely thirsty for psychic and spiritual experience. They are intelligent, dynamic, generally responsible, and enthusiastic about anything with an occult flavor. That my picture of them should not appear overly rosy, let me add that they also display a tendency toward over-argumentation and over-intellectualization.[^2]

Our new Margiis hired a professional translator, who was regarded as the foremost English speaker of Khabarovsk. When we requested her to join our meditation, she hesitated and said, “I don’t really think I am capable of such a thing.” Eventually she did sit with us, for a full hour. Afterward she said, “There must be something wrong with me. I couldn’t help crying throughout almost the entire meditation.” Imagine, this lady who did not have the slightest Tantric knowledge, already experienced an awakening of the spiritual force in her spine.

I remembered what Baba told me during a walk in Germany in 1979: “In the future, big spiritual festivals will be held in both Moscow and Beijing.” At the time I could not conceive how our programs could be openly held in the two countries which most represented the suppression of spirituality.

### Why He did not allow me to meet Him in jail

While I was holding a class in Khabarovsk, Russia, during a one-month spiritual training for a large group of Margiis, a sister said, “It is clear to all of us that Dadas and Didis possess

[^2]: To make this picture even more complete, I should mention that I later worked in Mongolia. Though most Mongolians are not so desperate for spiritual knowledge like the Russians, they have a greater tendency toward a spiritually centered lifestyle. Before communism took over Mongolia, it was nicknamed “Little India” because of its affinity for spiritual culture. The people seem as simple as the renowned devotees, from Krishna’s time, who were cowherds.

immense devotion for Baba. Some of us also feel a little love for Him. But how can we ever hope to achieve a deep communion with guru, when we are unable to meet Him? You were lucky. We are unlucky.”

Before replying, I momentarily thought of Baba, as I always try to do before any action, and, without expecting it, a flood of energy rose in my spine. All my cells tingled as the answer thrust itself before my eyes—an answer I had searched for, for so long.

There was that question that remained unanswered within me for so many years: *Why had He caused me such intense suffering by not allowing me to meet Him during my first seven years as a worker?* Why did He stop me that day I was determined to break into His Patna prison cell? Why did He pull me into a trance while I was sitting in the prison office, during which He psychically stroked my head and said, “There is a very good reason why I am not allowing you to meet me ... a very good reason.”

I finally knew the reason! It was for the sake of people who would never be able to physically meet Him, to whom I could talk about the deeply devotional connection I had with Baba over the seven long years before I was able to physically meet him.

I told my story to that group of Margiis, and then added, “Relation with guru is a purely spiritual matter. Meeting Him was beautiful, but it carries no guarantee. Though my words may sound trite, I can only repeat, the true Guru, the eternal lover, the eternal guide, the eternal One—is within you. But He is a jealous lover, who will only embrace you if you truly care for Him.”

### Midnight message

June 1995, Taichung, Taiwan. After half a lifetime of countless service projects—which either affected large numbers of people for a short time, or small numbers for a long time—I felt a strong urge to do something which would help

large numbers of people for a long time. Three Margiis joined me to research and figure it out.

Over days and weeks, we checked out the needs of orphans, poor alcoholic mountain people, elderly people, the physically disabled, and the mentally disabled. No use. Lots of organizations were already helping them—except the poor alcoholics in the mountains. Organizations had tried to help them, but gave up, because they wanted no help to change—they liked living as they were!

We wanted to be heroes, but found neither distressed maidens nor wretched souls. However, to do something at least, we ran frequent fasting camps, teaching natural therapy and detoxification.

Four months thus passed in research, research, and more research for the best service that we could do. We felt lost and directionless. Tension set in. Nerves frayed. I resolved to do forty nights of midnight kapalika meditation.

It was about the twentieth night of kapalika. While riding my motorcycle, suddenly, uncontrollably, all my suppressed frustration over-flowed. I roared at the top of my voice, “Baba, what kind of a guru are You? You’re useless! A simple guy with a simple need to serve, and what do you do? Zilch! I ask for nothing except a little itsy bitsy teeny weeny guidance. Hello, are You there? I’m fed up!”

Maybe it was the first time that I felt real anger with Baba. Philosophy states that any true feelings toward guru are good, even negative ones—because they bring the guru closer—but at this moment philosophy was light years away from me.

I still felt furious indignation as I arrived in the cemetery, and sat down to start meditation. A few minutes passed with anything-but-serene concentration.

Suddenly I heard His voice: “Do natural therapy as a service.”

Abruptly, as if a river of peace began flowing in my veins instead of blood, all my physical and psychic nerves relaxed, and I thought, “Ohhh yesss. That’s good.”

I laughed softly and whispered, “Thank You.”

And then added, “It was about time, Baba.”

The next morning, I mentioned to the staff that I had a crazy idea—to avoid their being influenced by my enthusiasm. Anyway, they loved it. So we immediately began working out how to create a natural therapy center.

At that time we had no idea that He was planning not one, but many centers.[^3]

### A different sort of midnight message

For a number of months, a part of my spiritual discipline was breaking down. After my very regular late-night kiirtan, I had developed the bad habit of lying down to do meditation instead of sitting up. Of course concentration was poor, and I usually spent the first hours of the night fluctuating between struggling to concentrate and simply falling asleep. The result was that I frittered away plenty of time and woke exhausted most mornings.

One night, in the midst of my usual time-wasting flow, I suddenly heard a loud voice say, “Sit up!”

Without thinking, I abruptly sat up. Baba was standing at my feet, saying, “Can’t you even sit up for ten minutes?”

I dived at His feet, and tried to kiss them. But in the same moment He disappeared, and I found myself kissing the air.

How bitter-sweet!

***

[^3]: Besides the global naturotherapy center in Cebu, at the time of this book coming to print, a good number of people have already started taking steps toward opening centers in their own countries—they have already completed the beginning stages of our training program, held their own yoga-detox camps, and are beginning to purchase or construct buildings.

In any case, it was a relief that He did not demand from me long late-night meditations. Since then I have never made the same mistake again.

### Midnight menace

In Hong Kong, I entered a graveyard for midnight meditation. I was attracted to an especially dark area with densely packed trees. Attracted because I felt something akin to a kind of fear I had not felt in a long time—it was tantalizing.

Walking among the trees and tombstones there, my tension increased. I found a gravestone with a bit of extra spooky feeling, and prepared it for my practice.

As soon as I closed my eyes to begin meditation, all the edginess completely disappeared. I felt deep peace and the meditation was superb.

Upon finishing my meditation and opening my eyes, however, the anxiety abruptly returned. I ignored it and continued to finalize the last steps of the procedure on the tombstone.

Suddenly my hand hit something on the stone which had not been there before I sat down.... Uggh. It was a large turd of excrement. The stool was either human or from a large animal.

It meant the tension I had been feeling was unquestionably due to an avidya Tantric.

“Come out and fight!” I shouted.

Again I shouted, this time moving in the direction of another part of the darkness. Immediately there was a hurried shifting in the shadows, and the sound of soft steps. I walked quickly to the spot.

I would like to say that our battle was hard-hitting, like a previous showdown I described, with another avidya Tantric. But I was too late.... The person who had been trying to either scare me or test his or her own power was gone.

### Troubles with the Mistake Master

February 1997, Seoul, South Korea. We had an on-going project collecting clothes, toys and school materials in Korea and shipping them to the needy in Siberia. At this stage I was spending half my time in South Korea and half in Siberia, Russia.

The head nun at a Won Buddhist temple agreed to store our clothes in between shippings.

One afternoon, she phoned me. “Dada, just thought I should remind you that today’s the last day before we need the space back.” It was a shock. I had been told that we had one more month.

I hung up the phone with a polite thank you. A normal person would have felt furious. Being a spiritual being, I simply felt tremendous intensity. My vocal cords then powerfully emitted the words, “Baba, You’ve done it again!”

The other Dada and LFT in the yoga center, both foreigners, jumped up, alarmed. “What happened, Dada?”

I said a few unrefined words, and then added, more to myself than to them, “We had one more month. I don’t know how He arranged that misunderstanding, but He did it beautifully.” Within hours, we needed to pack up mountains of clothes. We had no car, no boxes, were far from the temple, none of us spoke Korean, and all the Margiis who could have helped us were at work.

Without a thought of how the problem would be solved, I marched out on to the street, and headed in the direction of the train station. The other two tumbled after me.

Suddenly a car stopped next to us and, as had never happened before nor ever happened since, the driver rolled down his window and, seeing our orange uniforms, asked in passable English, “What are you people?”

Without breaking my pace, and without a word of explanation, I threw open his back door. His mouth fell open, and I said to my two co-actors, “Get in!”

They jumped in, as did I. “I request you not to just sit there,” I said to the amazed man. “Rather, we will all be gratified if you drive—quick!”

“But, ah, where are you going?”

“No time for chit chat. I’ll explain on the way. Now, please go!”

During the next six hours, our Korean driver translated our appeals to shopkeepers, helped us to collect scores of empty boxes and pack all the clothes. We finished, of course, just as the temple was locking up for the night.

Also, during that time he had decided to become a Margii.

As it was the new moon night, we headed for the graveyard. I initiated our new friend sitting on a tombstone.

### Who’s working?

During this period, while visiting India, I went to Ananda Nagar. On a hot morning, I walked with Ananda Marga’s president, Dada Shraddhananda, and his bodyguard down a dusty road.

“This weather reminds me,” I said, “of a summer day when DMC was about to begin here, and two Dadas spoke with each other within earshot of Baba. One of them said, really hinted, ‘It’s hot! Wouldn’t a little rain be nice?’”

I continued, “Of course Baba caught his meaning, and turned to him, saying, ‘This is not my duty. You know this is Kalikanandaji’s duty, and he always handles it nicely.’”[^4]

“Dadaji,” I then asked, “is Kalikanandaji still alive?”

“Yes, certainly. He was given a most important assignment by Baba. He and his band of sixteen Tantrics remain full time in the Himalayas, mostly immersed in meditation. Their daily task is to scan the world to see where there is either the possibility for any very good work to be done, or if there is any

[^4]: The reader will remember that when Baba was only sixteen years old, He initiated Kalikananda, who was until then an infamous criminal. Meditation turned him into a saint.

difficult problem confronting one of Baba’s workers—be they Margiis, wholetimers or anyone.

“He and his group then have the difficult job of creating and sending the vibrations or microvita which will enable the work to be done, or the problem to be solved.

“And to think we fret and fuss and worry so much about doing things which have already been done—and solving problems which have already been solved!”

He then grinned in such a mischievous way that I did not know whether he was smiling with me or at me.

### The tiger never sleeps

Dada Pranavananda told me that when he was in Mumbai he met Pradiip, a senior Margii, who told him of a dream he just had of Baba.

Baba was supposed to give darshan, but He was angry with everyone and unwilling. Suddenly Baba looked at Pradiip, and became even more furious. He shouted at Pradiip to take off his shirt, and then thrashed him heavily with His stick. Then Baba became normal and softly said, “Now I’m ready to give darshan.”

Pradiip felt a deep sense of peace, and woke up. But after waking, he did not remember dreaming.

A moment later, his wife saw that his back was covered with red welts. Surprisingly, there was no pain. She asked him the cause. Only then did he recall the dream.

Dada Pranavananda had a video camera with him, and filmed the stripes, which disappeared three days later.

### Blessing from a hidden yogi

On my last morning in Ananda Nagar, I meditated at my usual spot—under an old tree which Baba had once mentioned was the best *Tantra Piitha* (meditation power spot) in Ananda Nagar. Over the years I had introduced scores of overseas

Margiis to this place. All of them felt its power, sometimes in extraordinary ways.

This particular morning I was alone. Meditation was excellent, but I had to prepare for departure, and so cut it a bit short.

I started to walk away, when a force gripped me which I had never felt before. Without thinking, I walked back to the tree, opened my arms wide, and embraced it. The experience was shocking. I felt a huge energy pulsing through my entire body. It would have been frightening except for the fact that I loved this tree.

After a couple of minutes or so, normal consciousness started to return, and I remembered that my train was leaving soon. Slowly separating myself from the tree, I did namaskar as I backed away step by step.

I felt like the tree had given me something immensely beautiful—though I did not know precisely what it was.

Soon afterward, I left Ananda Nagar and also India, and returned to South Korea.

About two months later, I heard very sad news from a visiting Dada. A non-Margii farmer in Ananda Nagar had cut down that tree for firewood. The news stunned me.

“Why do you weep?” Dada asked me.

I told him about my experiences with that tree, including what happened on departure.

“Interesting,” he said. “Once in southern India I was with Baba on a field walk when we came to a gigantic tree with fields around it which no one wanted to cultivate. Farmers believed the tree was haunted.

“Baba told us that tree had been a great yogi in its past life. Unfortunately, the yogi misused his occult powers for selfish purposes, sometimes harming others. As a result, he was reborn as this tree. The yogi, as a tree, was still meditating. The vibration of his mantra created a peculiar vibration, and so people were afraid to go near.

“Baba told us all to sit for meditation. It became deeply silent. Then all of us did in fact hear a low pitched sound coming from the tree.

“Baba told us that after one month the tree would be reborn as a human again.

“I checked two months later. The tree was gone! Local people told me the tree had been hit by lightning, and was burned to ashes.”

### Timely donations—always

From 1995 to 1998, we started three projects—a basic natural therapy clinic in Taichung; a specialized natural therapy clinic inside of Taiwan’s largest elderly people’s residential community (of 400 people); and a small natural therapy in-patient facility on our organic farm in Jeonju, South Korea.

The in-patient facility provided treatment for people who each stayed there between one to four weeks. I also diagnosed and prescribed for thousands of out-patients at our fasting camps and in our centers. Each of these works and projects only lasted some years, but were stepping stones toward making something bigger and really long-term.

I also obtained a degree in naturopathy from a college in Calcutta.

In the year 2000, I chose Cebu, Philippines, to finally make a global, model natural healing and training center. The Ananda Marga group in Cebu, however, was very tiny, had no yoga center in the city, and so was not ready for such a project. Time was needed to first create and develop a base.

Three years later, when the new yoga center was already running with reasonably good success, I was in the Singapore airport—walking briskly because I was a bit late for a flight.

While contemplating nothing in particular, a question suddenly popped up from nowhere—“Are we ready? Is this the

right time to begin collecting funds for building the healing center?”

That same moment, my eyes spotted something on the airport floor. Still walking speedily, without breaking my pace, I bent down, picked it up and said loudly, “Did anyone drop anything valuable?” No one seemed interested in my words. So I held it high in the air and said, “Thank you, my Supreme Benefactor!” And continued on to catch my flight.

It was a US$100 note, the first donation for construction, obviously given by a certain Shrii Shrii Anandamurti.

After that first donation, some months later, we had, bit by bit, collected $10,000, and in November 2003, we started constructing a unique 22-room dome.

One fine day, five months later, the assistant architect announced to both the head architect and me: “Sorry, but I have to quit. It’s clear that Dada doesn’t have enough money for the building, it’s going to cost maybe $150,000, and I don’t see how he’s going to get it. I don’t feel like working for a project which is almost sure to fail.”

Saying good luck, he left.

The head architect, a 70-year-old German, turned to me, saying, “I should also quit, Dada.”

I looked down at the floor, wondering how Baba would solve this problem.

Then the German added, “But as you have already publicized it in the newspaper with my name, Ich bin ein Idiot—but I’ll continue until your funds run out.”

One and a half years later, I was invited to a Rotary Club meeting to lecture on naturopathy.[^1] It happened that the architect was there. Before I started the speech, I requested him to speak a few words about our soon-to-be-completed center.

[^1]: At the end of such lectures, I always welcomed the audience to make appointments for free personal consultations.

He said, “Ananda Marga and their Dada work as I’ve never seen before. For example, I remember when I made a big impression on the mayor. I handed him a six-page letter. Suddenly he soured because it was a detailed complaint about the excessive delays in our getting a building permit, and perhaps because of a few forceful words I spoke. So he unceremoniously declared, ‘This project is now rejected,’ and threw me out of his office.

“You cannot imagine how crushed I felt, how heavy my heart was as I drove to Dada’s yoga center. When I finally told Dada that the project was finished, I felt I would any moment start weeping. It was very sad.

“And then he shocked me. He rubbed my shoulder and said, ‘Don’t worry. The nobler the work, the greater the obstacles. It is a fact of life. We will work this out.’

“Honestly, I wasn’t comforted because I was sure there was no solution. Anyway, it took Dada two months, he looked around, found a childhood friend of mayor, cured him of diabetes, and that was that.

“But the one thing I’ll definitely never understand is how Dada and his group get their money. I was sure they could never get enough money, because they started with only a small fraction of what was needed. I tell you what they have been doing is simply impossible. It’s been nothing short of a constant miracle.”

Every month our expenses had been between US$4000 to $8000. And every penny had come by donations.

Though we never knew who would donate how much nor when any donations would come, the building work never paused. By the end of the 23 months of construction, over 1000 people had contributed, almost all of them because of benefits they had felt from natural therapy. This in itself was a great testimony to the effectiveness of natural therapy—and to the effectiveness of surrendering to the guru’s will, no matter what the difficulties.

In all work I am guided by Baba’s words, “When you are convinced that an action will be good, you should throw yourself into action then and there. You are destined to be victorious. Hesitation will be absolutely useless. In this way it becomes easy to perform righteous deeds.”

### The greatest movie ever

At a lecture I gave about meditation, a young man in the audience asked, “You’ve been practicing meditation for a very long time. Earlier you told us you’re not really sure what self-realization is. But I want self-realization. So why should I practice meditation?”

“Excellent question,” I said. “Before trying to answer, I’d like us to first take a vote on the most popular movie ever made.”

After a little surprise at the diversion, the audience made a list of movies, and then voted. The results were *Lord of the Rings* and *Star Wars*.

Then I asked everyone: “What makes such a film enjoyable?” After a little discussion, they agreed it was a spiritually-guided hero aiming for a great goal, who repeatedly overcame increasing challenges—even in the midst of deep darkness and uncertainty.

“As to meditation,” I said, “it will give you whatever you really want. If you want peace of mind, or if you want to sleep better, or if you want will power—whatever you really want, you will get by doing meditation.

“But if you really want self-realization—that’s spectacular. There’s nothing more enjoyable than that sort of noble struggle, in which the ultimate victory and knowledge remain shrouded in mystery.

“Of course the script of your self-realization movie is not only about meditation. It’s also got to be about fulfilling the greatest social purpose you can find—because infinite Being is not limited just to the inside of your mind.

“I am attempting to walk such a path, full of all its pitfalls, striving to be a hero inside of my own drama. And though I have an educated guess where it is going, I won’t really know until I get there. How wonderful. How exquisite!”

### Fruit falls from the Tree only when ripe

July 2008. Usually I did not mind difficulties too much, and used to sometimes say, “You’re welcome to give me your problems. Anyway, I eat problems for breakfast, lunch and dinner.”

This day, however, was one of those days when all sorts of crises joined together in the same moment.

Though the Ananda Marga Wellness Center had been running nearly full speed for well over two years, and though we had cured hundreds of so-called incurable cases including cancer, blocked arteries, diabetes, arthritis, stubborn skin diseases and high blood pressure—nevertheless, there had been endless administrative challenges every day. The foremost of those challenges, the crux of the problem, was that until then we had never found a proper manager. Nobody had the right guts for it.

So management problems kept falling in my lap, even though I had many other responsibilities besides the Wellness Center. And while it is always a spiritualist’s duty to be gleeful no matter how many complications simultaneously compete for his attention, I had become so busy that it was incessantly over the top—yes, it was quite too much of a good thing.

And most importantly, I knew that the Center could never fully succeed as long as it was dependent on a Dada. It needed to really be run by its staff.

Dada Viitashokananda, who was trained as a medical doctor, was temporarily with us, doing his best to help.

I walked into his room to talk. I wanted to tell him the problems, but also did not want to tell him.

Suddenly my bottled up thoughts hit the roof.

“Baba is overly cute! He knows we need a good manager, but He just goes on bringing people here who can’t hack it. How long will this silly drama go on?” Sparks of annoyance flying in all directions, I turned away from Dada, and said to Baba, “It’s not funny anymore! Yes, I’m sure You think You’re adorable, but...!”

Viitashokanandaji opened his eyes wider than usual, and shifted from foot to foot. He opened his mouth, pretended to say something inaudible, and thus nicely said nothing.

Though I appreciated that, it did not quite untie the knot.

I went up to my room, making every effort to remember that He was nicely enjoying Himself.

Then I noticed it. My handphone was blinking with an SMS message. It was from another Dada.

It said, “R u lookin 4 staff? Mayb I know some1.”

I hesitated, wondering if Baba was joking or trying to further torture me.

Or maybe, just maybe, He felt that I had done everything in my capacity to find a proper person, and that my samskara of seeking and not finding was finally burned.

Two phone calls led to an experienced professional social worker living 400 kilometers away. And not long after she came, we succeeded to put together an ensemble of staff who, to this day, all work together beautifully—lovingly and efficiently.

And this greatest of problems, like all others, was solved. For myself I can say with certainty—problems are solved every time by the Cosmic flow.

All the same, sometimes He needs a little final nudge, or even on occasion a no-nonsense jab.

### From Levis to levitation and beyond

Not long ago, a few Dadas and I were conversing about levitation.

Dada Liilananda said, “In 1978, when Baba was in jail, a bunch of us were arrested together and kept for some weeks in a different jail in Patna. The spiritual flow was fantastic. Sometimes during our kiirtans, I saw Dada S dancing off the ground, in the air. I thought about asking him to fly us out of the jail, but decided against it because we were having such a good time.”

That made me recall something. I said, “On the one-year anniversary of Baba’s physical departure, we had a six-day round-the-clock kiirtan in Calcutta. Hundreds, even thousands of us were dancing together. When our kundalinis were jumping up through our chakras, we were either non-stop laughing, or non-stop crying with ecstasy. At the center of the whirlpool, I also noticed the same Dada S dancing on air. He hopped back and forth like a skipping child, probably having no idea that his feet never reached the ground. He was floating two or three inches above the floor. I never told him nor anyone else because I didn’t want to inflate his ego.”

Then I laughed, “Yes, and now that we’re talking about it, I remember another similarly amusing incident. It was a few years ago. I was sitting in the meditation room in Taipei. Dada D was the only other person in the room. He sat right in front of me. Our meditation went on for a long time, and just when it was dipping very deep, I suddenly heard and felt a bang, as the floor shook. Dada D let out a groan. I opened my eyes and saw nothing special. I was curious, but closed my eyes and continued.

“Some minutes later, again the air became very still and the feeling was, well, spellbinding. I could feel that something was happening and, without thinking, again opened my eyes. This time I saw Dada D floating in the air, some inches above the floor. A few seconds passed, and then he crashed down and groaned.

“After some time when we finished, I asked him how his meditation was. He said ‘Pretty good. Except there were a

couple of times that I suddenly had sharp pain at the bottom of my backbone.’ I laughed. When he asked me what was funny, I told him what I had seen.”

I concluded, “On the same line, about a year after that, Dada D said to me, ‘It’s ironic. I’ll admit something to you. I used to sort of want occult power. The irony is now that I’ve got it, of course I can’t use it because it would just knock me off the path.’”

A journalist asked me during an interview, “Dada, what kind of extraordinary spiritual powers do you have?”

I answered, “A sincere meditator won’t use occult powers because they dilute and then eat up one’s energy, and make it impossible to achieve self-realization.

“There is only one power which I intentionally culture, and encourage others to also do. When an idea comes to do some great service work, we should embrace it fully. Then even if we run out of money, or volunteers quit, or the police block us—no matter how many times disaster looks certain, no matter how many people criticize or abandon us in the process—we don’t give up that work until we can say, ‘Hooray, it’s done!’ So every good work gets finished. That’s power.”

One of the mystic master’s jobs is to throw all sorts of broken glass and blazing hurdles on the roads leading to our targets. He does it consistently. After using these stumbling blocks to empower our otherwise weak nerves, He also has the job to finally see us succeed with each and every target. At both of these jobs, He never fails.

Including the ultimate target.

> He’s the scenic bridge from here to there.
> He has nothing else to do.
> Yes!

# Appendices

I. A Brief History of Ananda Marga
II. Introduction to the Progressive Utilization Theory (Prout)
III. Technical Talks by Baba or with Baba
IV. Tantra, Veda and Yoga

## I. A Brief History of Ananda Marga

This short history is from the notes I made at a talk given by Dada Vijayananda in Calcutta on the tenth anniversary of Baba's release. It explains a little of Ananda Marga's history up to the time of His release:

From 1955 to 1961, there were few rules in the organization. All the acharyas were family men and women. The work had only a little speed—not great speed. Nevertheless, B.M. Malik of the Central Bureau of Investigation wrote in his book, "I was instructed by Nehru to be vigilant on two organizations and on a particular man. The organizations were the Communist Party and the Jan Sangh. And the man was P.R. Sarkar." That was in 1955, at Ananda Marga's inception.

In 1961, the first training center was opened. The speed began to accelerate. The first avadhuta was initiated on the 19th of May 1963—Baba's birthday. At that time the *trishula* (three-pronged pitch-fork of Shiva) was carried by avadhutas, and only later was the *lathi* (fighting stick) introduced.

In the early years, I asked Baba if He would have Ananda Marga do service activities or not. He said, "Wait. All will come." Then, at the 1963 DMC, Baba took some tattered pieces of paper from His pocket. It was the Ananda Marga blueprint He had written at the age of sixteen (twenty-six years before). From that paper, He declared the beginning of the Education, Relief and Welfare Section (ERAWS). Then He

said, “Do something.” But for six months, no one had any clear idea what to do.

At that time, Baba’s mother did not want Him to write at night, so He had to write in secret by flashlight. In that way, He wrote the ERAWS guidebook. Then we understood what to do. Accordingly, on 1st January 1964, the first two grammar schools were opened in Jamalpur and Ananda Nagar.

Over the years, Ananda Marga started more than 1500 schools. But that first opening ceremony in Jamalpur was simply performed in the presence of the Service Department volunteers, one mother, two students and one snake. Due to the snake, the next day they brought cots for sleeping.

On 5th March 1967, came the attack on Ananda Nagar during which five of our workers were killed. After that, the Communists started a strong vilification campaign against Ananda Marga. They told the Hindus that Ananda Marga was from East Bengal, and was a Muslim organization. They told the Muslims that Ananda Margiis were fanatic Hindus. They told the tribals that Ananda Marga had come to take their land. They killed goats and pigs to show that Ananda Margiis ate meat.

But young people understood our vitality. Two years later, eighteen of our opponents were put in jail for culpability in the Ananda Nagar incident. From that point many began to understand that Ananda Marga was very strong.

Mr Roy and three other important barristers made an alliance against Ananda Marga. They sent many letters to Indira Gandhi to poison Ananda Marga’s reputation. They stressed, “This man, P.R. Sarkar, is dangerous, knows many things and can do much mischief. He is building a powerful organization. So no time should be wasted in striking against him. Yet remember, if you want to make a case against Ananda Marga, you must be very careful, because the Margiis always find the loopholes.”

On 29th December 1971, eighteen charges and six murder cases were filed against Ananda Marga. The following points

were highlighted:
1) Ananda Margiis believe in violence.
2) The source of their funds is doubtful.
3) Ananda Marga has connections with the American CIA.
4) Ananda Margiis are not morally honest.

All eighteen charges were dismissed from the court, and the six murder cases remained. Ultimately we were exonerated on all those cases in 1978.

Baba’s fast lasted five years, three months and two days. During long stretches of that time He took only orange juice twice daily; and during much of the time, during the so-called Emergency rule of martial law, He took only water. There was a period when He drank only two cups of malt drink daily. After martial law ended, He took two cups of curd water daily.

The then prison superintendent told me, “Baba is too extraordinary. Napoleon could be tackled in jail, but this living tiger cannot be dealt with.” Baba gave all the jail officials a hot time, demanding that they perform their duties properly. The top prison official came to see Baba, thinking that it might be his only chance to see this great yogi. Baba covered Himself fully with a cloth, so the officer was not even able to see Baba’s face. Baba charged him, “You are the Jail Magistrate who received my letter, but you took no step on my complaint.” The official was embarrassed. Then Baba started calling him names like “filth, swine, rubbish fellow” and so on. The man became so angry he had to rush outside, breathing heavily.

When announcement of the life-sentence verdict against Baba was given, He was on a stretcher. He simply smiled and said, “Now the tables will soon turn.” One month after that, Indira Gandhi decided to hold general elections. It was her greatest misjudgment. A convulsion moved throughout the country because so many poor, innocent people had suffered under martial law. Especially terrible was Sanjay Gandhi’s program of forced sterilization of sixteen-year-old slum boys. Mrs Gandhi’s opposition, the Janata Party, received the vast

majority of the vote. Late that night, Mrs Gandhi called her three top military chiefs, and ordered them to continue the Emergency. But they recognized what consequences could follow, and refused to comply.

Soon afterward, many of the court cases that had been decided during the period of martial law were reviewed, including our case. The court’s decision then fully exonerated Baba and the other Margiis of all charges.

On 3rd August 1978, at 6:00 a.m., all the offices and shops within six to seven kilometers of the Bankipur Jail in Patna were closed. About 7000 Margiis had arrived the previous night, and were joined by another 10,000. This sea of people clogged the streets for two kilometers.

At 1:10 p.m., Baba was brought out on a stretcher. His weight had dropped from 72 kilograms to 46 kilograms. He had also performed a yogi’s feat of suspended animation. The usual yogi who lives on air does not move at all. Excepting a stretch of eight days, Baba did not restrict His movements—though for one year He did not speak, and only wrote on a board. After His release, He remained four months in a wheelchair; His ankles and knees were too weak to provide support.

### II. Introduction to the Progressive Utilization Theory (Prout)

Prout is such a vast concept that a reasonable introduction to it requires an entire book. Baba’s series, *Prout in a Nutshell*, consists of over twenty books, and yet, as its title indicates, the entire series provides only fundamental principles and policies with examples. To attempt to explain Prout in an appendix is an injustice to the theory. Nevertheless, so many references are made to Prout throughout this book that the reader deserves some sort of explanation.

1) Prout is based on Shrii P.R. Sarkar’s concept of *Neo-humanism*, advocating a society concerned not only for human beings, but also for animals, plants, and inanimate resources. Neo-humanism does not accept narrow groupisms which function as social and psychological causes of exploitation. Such groups condemn and work against those who are outside of their own territory, clan, or belief system.

   Exploitative political power groups and selfish economic interest groups commonly dominate others through pseudo-culture and the mass media. As a result, the life of the entire planet is threatened by massive ecological destruction in the name of progress. Education and people’s movements should help expose the superstitions and dogmas which feed such narrow-mindedness. For this purpose, it is also needed to awaken benevolent human conscience and the capacity for rational analysis. Ultimately Neo-humanism aims at love for one and all, a state which is founded on spiritual enlightenment.

2) There should be maximum utilization, rational distribution and proper balance of all physical, mental and spiritual resources. Hence the name: Progressive Utilization Theory.

3) At any given time society is dominated by a specific psychological outlook or psychological class. If any class becomes over-entrenched in power, it tends to suppress progressive changes. Prout has a system of checks and balances which discourages this tendency, and encourages a forward shift in power whenever it is needed.

4) Economic planning should be decentralized as far as is practical. The most important planning is on the lowest level—the block level, that is, an area having about 100,000 people, having similar cultural and ecological concerns. Lower levels should be coordinated by higher levels, for example, blocks by states, states by regions, and regions by nations. Coordination represents concerns of trade, transport, communications, etc. The basis of boundaries between governing areas should gradually change from historical or political bias, to economic, ecological and cultural factors.

5) All levels of planning should follow certain economic principles including the following:
* Everyone must be guaranteed the purchasing capacity for their minimal necessities, including food, shelter, clothing, medical care and education.
* Opportunities for employment must be provided to all.
* There should be a progressive incentive system to encourage initiative, creativity and labor. Incentives may be given not only in the form of money, but also in kind. They may include, for example, equipment by which a person can improve performance, improved working facilities, household items, or other amenities. Incentives may also be provided through special education, new job possibilities, increased free time, cultural opportunities, recreational facilities, and so on.
* There may always be a gap between the minimum and

maximum economic levels. The economic vitality of a society, however, is judged by the speed of _improvement in the minimum standard_ of living. In a healthy society, the gap is gradually diminishing.
* Improvement in _technology_ is beneficial to mankind when it results in neither ecological imbalance nor loss of employment. Due to improved mechanization, workers should benefit whenever possible by diminishment of working hours without loss of salary.
* Ignoring _psychological factors_ at the working place is harmful to both the workers and their output. Therefore a portion of the budget and management time must be allocated to ensure cleanliness, safety, equability, ecological care, and an atmosphere which encourages initiative and creativity.

6) These planning principles should be implemented within the frame-work of _self-sufficient economic zones_. These zones may be created by blocks and regions joining together on the basis of economic potentiality, ethnic similarity, and environmental features. When the local people are directly working for their zone's economic well-being and self-sufficiency, then they become highly motivated. The local government must plan for a cooperative economy between agriculture, manufacturing, trade, commerce and professional services. The government will naturally be concerned for maintaining long-term fertility of its soil and all-round ecological balance

7) Such economic zones prefer the _development of local resources_ in lieu of exporting raw materials or importing finished products. This policy encourages the growth of a healthy indigenous manufacturing sector, full employment, and greater security in times of difficulty.

8) Within each zone there should be a _three-tiered structure_ of economic enterprises.
* _Cooperatives_ constitute the largest sector, including all agriculture and most industries and commercial companies. They are owned and managed by their workers. They should be profitable and competitive.
* _Key industries_ provide essential resources such as energy, minerals, large-scale transport, and communication. They are managed as public utilities by the relevant economic level of the government. Boards of directors are elected democratically, and workers participate in management.
* _Private ventures_ include small-scale industries, shops and services.

9) Through the inspiration provided by the multifarious incentive system, through cooperative ownership, and through economic zonal planning, _safeguards_ are created _against over-accumulation_ of wealth in which vested interests hoard resources.

10) _Zonal planning_ should see that villages have sufficient cleanliness, natural beauty, wildlife, and cultural and educational facilities so that all people, animals and plants will enjoy the process of decentralization. Over-populated cities must become a thing of the past.

11) Prout is _not a utopian philosophy_. Society is the field for everyone's development, and therefore imperfections will always exist. Prout recognizes the need for checks and balances on individual power. _High-caliber leadership_ consists of persons having certain qualities such as
* a reputation for honesty and wisdom
* a history of community service
* demonstrated skills in leadership, management and technical knowledge.

Such leaders should be elected democratically. _Democracy_, however, is only successful according to the mentality of the voters. Therefore a literate, well-educated and well-informed electorate is essential. Accordingly, the influence of the mass media and wealth in politics must be regulated.

12) One of the fundamental works in establishing Prout is to stimulate well-intentioned and capable people to participate in the leadership process. It is commonly believed that power corrupts, and more power corrupts more. If potential Prout leaders engage themselves in intensive social work, healthy living, sacrifice for others, relevant technical education, and spiritual practices—then they may _develop the incorruptible qualities_ needed in the leaders of a society based on coordinated cooperation.

13) Prout supports the _eventual establishment of a representative world government_. Only when small countries are free of the threat of domination by big countries will global cooperation be possible. Solving global problems depends on establishing local economic security. Political freedom without economic independence has no value. Hence Prout's policy is economic decentralization and centralized political cooperation.

14) Useful theory is generally only evolved on the basis of _practical experimentation_. Therefore the first steps in expanding the Prout theory result from attempts to implement it in communities, such as Master Units. As Master Units merge their concerns with the surrounding neighborhoods and villages, Prout theory is developed and demonstrated. Practical experience is also gained through organizing public education programs and social movements. Such movements support indigenous culture,

work for ecological improvement, and struggle against psycho-economic exploitation.

### III. *Technical Talks by Baba or with Baba*
